# Sūrah Ali 'Imran (Chapter 3) — Verse-by-Verse Tafsir

## Introduction to the Sūrah

Sūrah Āl ʿImrān takes its name from the household of ʿImrān, the father of Maryam, whose story is told in its middle verses. It is one of the longest chapters of the Qur'an, two hundred verses, and it was revealed in Madīnah. Al-Qurṭubī records the agreement of the community on that point, and al-Baghawī opens his commentary on the sūrah with the same word. The chapter belongs, then, to the Madinan period, when the community was established in a city of its own and the arguments with the People of the Book were being settled in public.

Ibn Kathīr ties the opening of the sūrah to a specific event. The first eighty-three verses answer the Christian delegation from Najran, which came to Madīnah in the ninth year after the migration and argued about the nature of ʿĪsā. That setting explains the shape of the chapter's first third: it establishes the oneness of God, denies that He has a son, tells the story of the family of ʿImrān, and ends with the passage in which the two sides are invited to call down a curse on whichever of them is lying.

Al-Qurṭubī adds, on al-Naqqāsh's authority, that the sūrah is named Ṭaybah in the earlier books. Ibn Kathīr notes that its virtues were treated together with those of al-Baqarah at the head of that sūrah's commentary, and the pairing is natural: al-Baqarah lays down the law for the new community, and Āl ʿImrān answers the learned objections brought to it.

The chapter's order is deliberate. It opens with the letters that stand alone, then with the declaration of God's oneness, then with the books He sent down and the measure by which claims are weighed. From there it turns to the two groups who met the message: those who rejected it and those who believed, and it tells the story of the family from which ʿĪsā came, including the birth of Maryam, her service in the sanctuary, the gift of Yahya to Zakariyyā, and the truth about ʿĪsā himself. The middle of the sūrah turns to the community's own testing at Uḥud, where a setback was allowed to teach the believers the difference between loyalty and convenience. The last part of the chapter returns to the People of the Book, to the covenant they were given, and to the money their scholars took in exchange for passing over what they knew. The sūrah closes by telling the believers what to do while the argument continues: be patient, stand firm, and remain mindful of God, so that the community may succeed.

Where al-Baqarah gives a community its law, Āl ʿImrān forms its character under pressure. Its most repeated theme is that truth is not carried by numbers: a small band can defeat a large one, a setback can be a mercy, and the reward of a believing servant is not measured by whether the present hour goes his way. The verses on Uḥud carry that lesson in the plainest language in the whole Book, since they speak to a community that had just lost a battle and was inclined to blame everything except itself.

The opening of the sūrah is the key to the rest. Before the story of the family, before Uḥud, and before the arguments with the People of the Book, the chapter settles who God is and what He has sent. Those who read on will find the same two names, the Ever-Living and the All-Sustaining, opening al-Baqarah's greatest verse, and Ibn Kathīr records the report that God's greatest name is contained in the passage at the head of this sūrah and in the like of it there.

## Verse 3:1

> Alif-Lãm-Mĩm

**THE LETTERS AT THE HEAD OF THIS SŪRAH**

The sūrah begins with three letters standing alone before any sentence of the Book, and a reader meets ***“Alif-Lãm-Mĩm”*** at the head of the chapter. The pause after them belongs to the reading itself. Al-Jalālayn sums the whole matter up in one line and leaves it there, and the reason for the brevity is that the letters carry no claim a reader can weigh on his own. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān classes them among the verses whose meaning is left with God, and notes that this was the position the early community kept.

The letters are pronounced one by one, each name given in full rather than joined into a word. Since they are not built like ordinary words, the rules that measure a single name do not apply to them. Al-Qurṭubī records the old argument over the pause that follows: whether the reader stops on the final letter or carries it into the next word. The grammarians differed, and the reading of the cities settled the question for the community. What follows from the disagreement is small and important: the Chapter was received as it is, without the early readers repairing its openings.

**AN OATH BEFORE ANY CLAIM IS MADE**

One of the earliest readings of the letters comes through Ibn ʿAbbās, and it takes them as an oath by which God swore that He is One, with no child and no partner. That reading explains why the letters stand where they do, since the sūrah is about to answer a people who held that God had a son. The oath comes before the argument the way a judge states the law before hearing the case.

The same report offers another reading: the letters announced the account of the delegation itself. On that view, the chapter declares its subject before it declares anything else, and this is why the material that follows reads like an answer to a known question rather than a general sermon. Al-Ālūsī lists several further attempts from the early scholars, some taking the letters as names of God and others as the openings of words. He gives each its due and then declines to choose. The point is not which guess is cleverest. The point is that the community kept the letters as they came down, and no reading of them was ever made a condition of faith.

**THE DELEGATION THAT CAME TO MADĪNAH**

The chapter was revealed in Madīnah, and al-Qurṭubī records the agreement of the community on that. He adds, on al-Naqqāsh's authority, that the sūrah is named Ṭaybah in the earlier books. Since the setting is Madīnah and the audience includes learned visitors from outside it, the arguments in the opening passages are aimed at people who knew the earlier scriptures well. Ibn Kathīr gives the date: the first eighty-three verses answer the Christian delegation from Najran, which reached Madīnah in the ninth year after the migration.

Al-Baghawī sets the scene in detail. Sixty riders came, fourteen of them nobles, and three of the fourteen carried the weight. Al-ʿĀqib, whose name was ʿAbd al-Masīḥ, was their commander in the field and the one whose counsel they would not leave. Al-Sayyid, whose name was al-Ayham, fitted them out for the road. Abū Ḥārithah b. ʿAlqamah was their bishop and their scholar. They entered the mosque after the afternoon prayer, wearing cloaks of Yemeni cloth. Those who saw them said they had never seen a delegation like them. When the time of their own prayer came, they stood in the mosque and faced the east, and they were left to it. That permission is worth noticing, because it shows the kind of argument the sūrah is having: the visitors were allowed their worship, and then met with a case against their belief.

The exchange that followed is kept in full by al-Baghawī. The two leaders greeted him, and the answer was an invitation to submit. They replied that they had submitted long before, and the reply was that they had not. The obstacles were then named: the claim that God has a son, the worship of the cross, and the eating of pork. Their answer was a question they thought unanswerable. If ʿĪsā is not a son of God, then whose son is he? That question is the hinge of the whole opening, because the sūrah will answer it in two steps: first with who God is, then with the truth about ʿĪsā himself.

Child after child dies in a village where the water is bad, and the elders argue about which well is at fault. Their argument cannot be settled until the water itself is tested. The sūrah works the same way. Before it answers the delegation about ʿĪsā, it settles who God is, and the answer comes back to that question at every turn. The letters stand in front of the reply like a seal on a folded letter, and the reply begins with the next sentence.

---

## Verse 3:2

> Allah! There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him—the Ever-Living, All-Sustaining

**THE SENTENCE THAT OPENS THE CHAPTER**

The declaration that follows the letters is a statement of oneness, and everything after it in the sūrah rests on it. Al-Jalālayn gives the plain sense of the words: ***“Allah! There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him”***, the Living, the Eternal. Ibn ʿAbbās fills the two names with meaning, since the Living is the One who does not die and does not pass away, and the Eternal is the One who stands by Himself and has no beginning. The sentence is not an argument yet. It is a statement laid down before the argument begins, and that order is deliberate, because a reader cannot weigh a case until he knows who the judge is.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān points out the arrangement the section keeps: the first verse presents a reasoned proof of God's oneness, and the verses that follow bring the proof from revelation. In other words, the sūrah argues from two directions at once, from what reason can see and from what God has said. The same chapter then answers the doubts of the delegation, one by one, in the order they were raised. A building is raised from the foundation upwards, which means no part of it is left hanging. Think of a bridge: the two names at the end of the sentence are its two supports, and the road of the chapter runs on both of them.

**WHAT THE TWO NAMES CARRY**

The sentence closes with ***“the Ever-Living, All-Sustaining”***, and the sources treat the pair as one answer to two questions. Al-Saʿdī explains the first name as life that is complete, without lack or interruption, and the second as the One who stands by Himself and keeps everything else standing. That is why the keeping is described so widely: it covers the affairs of religion, the affairs of daily life, and the decrees that run through both. Nothing is left to itself, and nothing is left half-kept.

Al-Ṭabarī records the readings of the two names at this point, including one from ʿUmar and Ibn Masʿūd and a shorter form from ʿAlqamah, and the reading of the cities is the one the community kept. The reports differ in the form and not in the sense, since every reading holds the same two meanings: life without end and care without pause. Al-Ālūsī takes up the grammar and asks why the two names are raised and why they are described with the definite article. The argument is technical, and the conclusion matters: the names are not titles pinned on at random, but a description that answers the declaration standing before them.

Ibn ʿUthaymīn teaches the same point from the other side: the One who keeps all things needs no one to keep Him. In other words, the two names lean on each other. The Life is a life that has no need, and the Sustaining is a care that comes from having no need. A ruler who is alive but helpless, or strong but unaware, is not what these names describe. What follows is a God who is neither absent nor dependent, and that is the floor the rest of the sūrah stands on.

The pair keeps a reader from a common slip. A man may trust in God's power while forgetting that His knowledge reaches everything, or speak of His knowledge while imagining that some matter escapes His hand. The two names shut both doors. Because the living God is not absent and the One who sustains the world is not careless, the warning that comes later in the chapter is not a threat thrown out at random.

**WHERE THE SAME PAIR STANDS AGAIN**

The passage was counted among the greatest statements of the Book. Ibn Kathīr records the report that God's greatest name is contained in these verses and in the like of them at the head of the throne verse in al-Baqarah, and the two places are read side by side. The wording there is the same: ***“(2:255 — **“the Ever-Living, All-Sustaining”**)***. Since the same description of God is repeated in two sūrahs, what is said here is not a local teaching for one group of listeners. It is the same declaration, repeated because the need for it is repeated.

The delegation from Najran argued from what they had seen: a man they honoured, born without a father. The sūrah answers with what God is. A man born without a father still needs food, sleep, protection and time. The One described here needs none of these, and the word God belongs to Him alone. Nothing in the sentence depends on the visitors' premises, and that is the point of placing it first. It stands on its own, and the verses that follow draw out what it means for ʿĪsā, for the earlier books, and for those who reject what has come down.

---

## Verse 3:3

> He has revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ the Book in truth, confirming what came before it, as He revealed the Torah and the Gospel

**WHAT GOD SENT DOWN BEFORE THIS**

The address moves from who God is to what He has sent, and the words ***“He has revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ the Book in truth”*** carry the whole claim of the chapter. Al-Ṭabarī reads the truth here as truth in what the Book reports and truth in what it decides, since the disputes of the People of the Book are settled by it and not by the weight of their disputants. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same sense in the form of a sending: God sent Gabriel with the Scripture so that truth would be separated from falsehood. On that reading the Book arrives as a separation, not as an addition, and this is why the chapter can put it forward as the measure of what came before.

The words ***“confirming what came before it”*** mean that the Book stands as a witness to what God sent earlier and to the men who carried it. Ibn ʿAbbās ties the confirming to God's oneness itself, since the earlier scriptures, on the reading he gives, taught the same oneness the Qur'an now declares. Ibn Kathīr names the two books that follow: the Torah is the book of Mūsā, and the Gospel is the book of ʿĪsā, and both are God's speech to His servants rather than the work of human hands. That is why a person who honours Mūsā and ʿĪsā should not be troubled by a book that agrees with them. What turns on the ordering is this: if the earlier books agree with the Qur'an about God, then the claim that God has a son was never part of what those books brought.

**ONE SENDING IN PORTIONS, TWO IN FULL**

The two earlier books are then named directly: ***“as He revealed the Torah”*** and ***“and the Gospel”***, sending the reader to books his own tradition already honours. Ibn ʿAbbās describes both sendings together, since the Torah came down in one piece to Mūsā b. ʿImrān and the Gospel in one piece to ʿĪsā b. Maryam. Al-Qurṭubī draws out the difference in the way the books arrived. The Qur'an came over time, piece after piece, as events required, while the two earlier books came each in one delivery, and the difference in the wording of the sending answers the difference in the way they came.

The reason for the two ways of sending is practical. A law given to a people at one moment suits a community that receives its whole order on one day, and a book given in portions suits a community being built over years, with its questions arriving as events arrive. In other words, the delivery fits the work each book was given to do. It is as though a household had been handed a map on the day it set out, and a later traveller received the same map with the roads marked more plainly. The country is one, and the second copy serves those who come after.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the consequence for the reader: nothing in the claim of this verse was new to the prophets before. The earlier scriptures taught the same oneness, and the Qur'an therefore confirms them rather than replacing them with a strange teaching. Al-Saʿdī puts the confirmation in its strongest form, since the Qur'an bore witness to the earlier books, agreed with them, and declared true the messengers who brought them. Because the message is one, the disagreement of the later communities was never about the message itself. It was about what they added to it, and the Book returns them to what was sent.

**THE ARGUMENT WITH THE DELEGATION**

The order of the verse matters for the visitors who came from Najran. They arrived with scriptures in their hands and a question about ʿĪsā on their tongues, and the chapter first establishes that the new Book confirms the old ones. That order makes the burden of proof theirs, since they must show that their claim about God's son came from the books they honour. The measure named later in the chapter, the Standard that separates right from wrong, is what settles the dispute.

Ibn Kathīr reads this verse with an eye on the exchange that follows, since the answer to the delegation includes the passage in which the two sides are invited to call down a curse on whichever of them is lying. The invitation is not a debating trick, because it rests on the confidence that the Book has brought the truth and the earlier books said the same. A man who knows his case does not fear a public test. The chapter writes that confidence into its third sentence, before the test is proposed, and the reader is meant to notice the order in which the two things come.

---

## Verse 3:4

> previously, as a guide for people, and ˹also˺ revealed the Standard ˹to distinguish between right and wrong˺. Surely those who reject Allah’s revelations will suffer a severe torment. For Allah is Almighty, capable of punishment

**THE PURPOSE OF THE EARLIER BOOKS**

The two books came ***“previously, as a guide”***, and that phrase carries their whole purpose. They were not sent as records to be admired, or as proof that God had spoken long ago, since guidance is given for the road a person is still walking. Al-Ṭabarī reads the phrase as a statement of direction: God sent them before the Qur'an, for the people of their own time, and the guidance reached the people it was sent to. Ibn Kathīr keeps the same reading and notes that guidance was the reason for every book God sent.

The word ***“for people, and ˹also˺ revealed the Standard ˹to distinguish between right and wrong˺. Surely those”*** ties the earlier books and the Standard into one sentence, and the tie is the point. The Standard is the name this verse gives to the Qur'an in the middle of a passage about the Torah and the Gospel. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān keeps the older books and the new one under the same care: the same hand sends them all, and the last of them judges what came before. Al-Saʿdī puts the seal on it, since with the Qur'an the message reaches its end and no new book will come after it. That is why the guide and the Standard are named in one breath. The guidance was always one thing, given in more than one copy.

**THE MEASURE THAT SEPARATES**

Ibn ʿAbbās explains the Standard as that which separates truth from falsehood, which means it is the measure by which a claim is weighed and either upheld or set aside. The title answers a practical problem, since both sides in a dispute can quote books. The Standard decides between the two quotations, and it decides because the One who sent it knows what He sent before.

Al-Baghawī keeps the exchange with the delegation in view at this point. The three leaders disputed about ʿĪsā with the Prophet ﷺ, and the dispute turned on the same question they had come with. Since the Standard is named in the middle of the reply, the reader sees what settles such a dispute: not the weight of the disputants, but the measure they are weighed against. The verse does not say that discussion is wrong. Discussion has an end, and that end is the Book.

The same name appears elsewhere in the Qur'an for the same book: (25:1 — **“the Standard”**), sent down so that its bearer may warn the whole world. In other words, the measure is not a private possession of one community. Because the Book judges what people differ over, it can speak to a delegation of Christians, a community of Jews, and a tribe of idolaters in the same city, and none of them is excused from the measure by his ancestry.

**THE COST OF REFUSAL**

The sentence then turns and names the cost of refusal: ***“who reject Allah’s revelations will suffer a severe torment”***. The rejection named here is not an honest failure to understand, since a guide and a measure have just been named. It is the refusal of what has been sent after the proof has come. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the warning follows the naming of the books on purpose, because a person who turns from the Torah and the Gospel in their true form, and then turns from the Qur'an as well, has refused guidance in every form it took.

The warning stands in the middle of a chapter that answers a specific delegation, and that setting gives it its force. Those visitors came to discuss, and some of them went away with their position changed. The verse does not treat the discussion as the danger. It treats the refusal that outlives the discussion as the danger, and the reason is that a refusal is not an opinion. It is a decision, made with the measure in front of the man who makes it.

**POWER THAT CARRIES OUT THE WARNING**

The verse ends with ***“For Allah is Almighty, capable of punishment”***, and the two words are placed there to close the door on a false comfort. A judge who is able to punish but cannot carry it out is no threat to a criminal, and a warning from such a judge would be noise. Al-Saʿdī explains the ending as a proof that the threatened punishment will come, since the power described is universal and nothing can resist it or turn it aside.

Al-Ālūsī adds the element of repetition, because the naming of God as Mighty and able to punish returns in the verses that follow. The repetition is like a knock at a door given more than once. A person who hears a warning once may take it as the language of a preacher. Hearing it again, with the reminder that the One who warns is able to carry out the warning, leaves the reader with the same choice the delegation faced, and the same time to answer it. That is what turns on the last two words of the verse: the warning is not finished when the sentence ends.

---

## Verse 3:5

> Surely nothing on earth or in the heavens is hidden from Allah

**NOTHING ESCAPES HIS KNOWLEDGE**

The next sentence names what no one can escape: ***“Surely nothing on earth”*** and ***“or in the heavens is hidden from Allah”***. Al-Jalālayn gives it in its plain form, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the older wording of the same point. Since there is no place a thing can be put where God will not see it, the sentence shuts every door a person might imagine for himself. Al-Saʿdī expands it to the widest reach: what is outward and what is inward, what came first and what comes last, the small and the great.

The sentence follows the description of God as Living and Sustaining, and the order teaches something. A keeper who does not know the state of what he keeps is not a keeper, which means the two names and this sentence belong together. Power without knowledge would be blind, and knowledge without power would be helpless. Think of a craftsman who knows every joint of the house he built: a crack in a wall he no longer visits is still a crack he made, and he still knows it. The same holds true of the whole creation.

**WHY THIS SENTENCE STANDS HERE**

The position of the sentence is not casual. The reader has just heard of books sent, of guidance given, and of a punishment promised to those who refuse. The question that follows is whether there is One who can see what each person does with the guidance, in every moment and every place. That is why the verse answers with knowledge rather than with power alone. Because the account of the gathering is exact, the record of what is gathered has to be exact too.

Al-Qurṭubī ties the sentence to the delegation as well, since the visitors argued in the open and the negotiations continued behind closed doors. Neither setting is out of view, and the same is true of the reader. A trader who underweighs in a closed shop, and a ruler who acts unjustly in a private room, are as visible to God as the two sides of the Najran debate were to each other. In other words, the sentence is a witness standing in every room.

Ibn ʿUthaymīn treats the sentence as a proof and a warning at once: a proof, because the One who knows everything is the One whose care reaches everything, and a warning, because nothing done in secret is beyond the account. Al-Ālūsī notes that the knowledge named here is what makes the sustaining complete, since a keeper who is unaware of a change cannot answer it. The difference between a watchman who sleeps and one who does not is the difference between a name and a reality.

The consequence for conduct follows plainly: the one who knows that God sees him does not need a witness in order to be honest, and the one who forgets it does not become hidden by being alone. That is the ground on which the rest of the chapter's arguments stand. The delegation could examine the Prophet ﷺ face to face, and the verse reminds every reader that the examination runs the other way as well. The sentence is short, and it carries the whole chapter.

---

## Verse 3:6

> He is the One Who shapes you in the wombs of your mothers as He wills. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him—the Almighty, All-Wise

**THE ONE WHO FORMS THE CHILD IN THE WOMB**

The sūrah turns from the Book to the formation of the child, and the turn is part of its answer to the delegation. The words ***“He is the One”*** place the work in the hands of God alone, and the sentence continues ***“Who shapes you in the wombs of your mothers”***, which means the shape of every person is decided before birth by the One who decided it. Ibn Kathīr explains the shaping as creation in the womb according to God's will, whether male or female, handsome or otherwise, happy or miserable. No one who reads the verse can claim a share in that work.

The women's womb is named in the plural, and the mercy in the name is not lost on al-Qurṭubī. He notes that the word for womb comes from the same root as mercy, since the womb is a place where people are shown mercy to one another. He also gives the older sense of the shaping: a form given to something so that it leans toward a shape it did not have before. Since the work begins before a child can ask for anything, no one earns his own face or his own frame.

**HOW THE VERSE ANSWERS THE DELEGATION**

The verse lands on the argument the chapter has been having. Al-Ṭabarī reads it as a declaration that clears God of any peer, and as a refutation of those who spoke about ʿĪsā in the way the delegation from Najran spoke. Ibn Kathīr draws the conclusion in plain words: ʿĪsā was created in a womb and shaped as God willed, which makes him a created servant like the rest of humanity. Since the same hand formed both the prophet and the people he was sent to, the claim that he shares in divinity collapses.

Al-Ālūsī treats the sentence as a new statement about God's sustaining care, and he points out that it shows knowledge attached to things before they exist. In other words, the shaping in the womb is proof that God's knowledge reaches what has not yet been made. That is why the verse can pass from knowledge in the previous sentence to creation in this one without changing its subject. The effect is a single argument: the One who knows what is hidden forms what He knows, and the One who forms all people cannot be one of them.

**THE NAMES THAT CLOSE THE SENTENCE**

The sentence ends with ***“as He wills. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him”***, and then with ***“the Almighty, All-Wise”***. Ibn ʿAbbās fills the two names with force: the Almighty is the One who is not overcome, and the Wise is the One whose shaping in the womb is not random. Al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense in a short line, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the two names answer the doubts raised about the earlier books, since power and wisdom are the two qualities that make a claim of divinity impossible for anyone else.

The names also answer the way people talk about children. A family waits for a child and hopes for a boy or a girl, a healthy body, a good mind. The shaping was never in the family's hands, since the One who decided it decided it with wisdom and carried it out with power. Think of a potter at his wheel who works without instructions from the clay. The clay has no say in the shape it receives, and the potter has no need of its advice.

What follows for the reader is a settled trust. Ibn ʿUthaymīn notes that the unborn child lies in darkness, unable to see, unable to speak, and that its food and its growth come through no effort of its own. Since the One who shaped it before it could ask for anything is able to provide for it after it is born, worry about the future is a kind of forgetting. The verse teaches the lesson before the need arrives. That is why it is placed after the description of God's knowledge: the knowledge that reaches the womb reaches the rest of a life as well.

---

## Verse 3:7

> He is the One Who has revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ the Book, of which some verses are precise—they are the foundation of the Book—while others are elusive. Those with deviant hearts follow the elusive verses seeking ˹to spread˺ doubt through their ˹false˺ interpretations—but none grasps their ˹full˺ meaning except Allah. As for those well-grounded in knowledge, they say, “We believe in this ˹Quran˺—it is all from our Lord.” But none will be mindful ˹of this˺ except people of reason

**THE CLEAR VERSES AND THE ELUSIVE ONES**

The Book is opened here, and its contents are divided. The verse begins ***“He is the One”*** and then ***“Who has revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ the Book, of”***, and the next words describe what is inside it: ***“which some verses are precise”***. Ibn Kathīr explains the precise verses as entirely clear and plain, open to every reader, and he identifies them as ***“they are the foundation of the Book”***, the ground on which its rulings are built. al-Baghawī keeps that sense, since the precise verses are distinct in meaning, their proofs visible, and they are acted upon without hesitation.

The other kind is named next: ***“while others are elusive. Those with deviant hearts follow the elusive verses seeking ˹to spread˺ doubt through their ˹false˺ interpretations”***. Al-Saʿdī explains that these verses carry more than one possible sense until they are read together with the precise ones, which is why they are a place where a crooked heart can find work. A person with an argument to defend does not go to the clear commands. He goes to the ambiguous phrase, since that is where he can press his own meaning into the text.

**THE HEARTS THAT CHASE WHAT THEY CANNOT CARRY**

The phrase ***“but none grasps their ˹full˺ meaning except Allah.”*** states where the search ends. Al-Ṭabarī takes the older debate over the pause at this point, and the question is whether the sentence closes with God alone or joins those grounded in knowledge to Him. His own reading stops the meaning with God, and he reports the reading of Ibn ʿAbbās that carries the well-grounded into the same clause. What turns on the difference is not the duty of study. It is whether a created mind can ever hold the full sense of a divine word, and al-Ṭabarī treats the answer as no.

Al-Qurṭubī reports that ʿĀʾishah, the wife of the Prophet ﷺ, said that he recited this verse and then said, *"When you see those who follow the elusive parts, know that these are the ones God has named, so beware of them."* Bukhārī and Muslim both carry the report. The line between study and sickness is drawn by desire, since both groups read the same verses. One comes to learn, and the other comes to win. That is why the text blames the following and not the reading, and the difference is visible in what a person does when his reading is answered.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān sets the verse against the argument of the delegation. The Christians took certain phrases and used them to prove that ʿĪsā shares in divinity, and the sūrah answers by naming the precise verses as the foundation and the elusive ones as the test. A reader who joins the two kinds reads the phrases in the light of the plain commands, and the plain commands give God no partner and no child. Since the Book explains itself, the way to the meaning of an unclear phrase is through the clear ones.

**THE POSITION OF THOSE WHO KNOW**

The verse then turns to the other group: ***“As for those well-grounded in knowledge, they say, “We believe in this ˹Quran˺”*** and ***“it is all from our Lord.””*** Ibn ʿAbbās describes them as the firmly established, whose knowledge does not turn them proud, and al-Baghawī notes that their answer is faith rather than a new interpretation. Their submission holds both kinds of verses at once, because they accept the source before they settle the sense. In other words, their knowledge raises the question and their trust carries them past it.

Ibn Kathīr draws out the lesson from their posture: knowledge that produces humility is the knowledge of the Book, and knowledge that produces debate for its own sake has already left it. Think of a man who trusts the physician who wrote his prescription, even where he cannot read the reason for every dose. He takes the medicine the way a doubter would not. The well-grounded reader treats the verses he understands as commands and the verses he does not as belonging to the same giver.

The verse closes by setting a limit on who benefits: ***“But none will be mindful ˹of this˺ except people of reason”***. Ibn ʿUthaymīn notes that the warning is not aimed at those who lack the capacity to understand, since the verse names reason as the tool the reader is expected to use. The mind is asked to work, and it is not asked to be the measure. What follows is a discipline rather than a puzzle: read the clear parts as law, bring the elusive parts to them, and leave the rest with the One who sent them down. Al-Ālūsī keeps the same reading of the passage and adds that the one who takes this path is not asked to stop thinking, since the verse itself is addressed to thinkers.

---

## Verse 3:8

> ˹They say,˺ “Our Lord! Do not let our hearts deviate after you have guided us. Grant us Your mercy. You are indeed the Giver ˹of all bounties˺

**THE PRAYER OF THOSE WHO KNOW**

The word in the text is that the well-grounded do not stop at a statement of belief. The verse opens ***“˹They say,˺ “Our Lord!”*** and then ***“Do not let our hearts deviate after you have guided us. Grant us Your mercy. You are”***, which means the people with the deepest knowledge are the people most afraid of losing the way. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the words carry an ellipsis, since the speech continues from the people already described, and he gives the sense of deviating as a turning away, a corruption of the heart's direction.

The prayer asks for firmness after guidance, and Ibn ʿAbbās explains it as a request to be kept firm upon the religion. He adds that the mercy asked for is a firm hold rather than a new gift, since the one who prays already stands inside the guidance he fears to lose. Al-Baghawī reads the same lines as a plea not to be turned away as others were turned, and he explains the mercy as success and steadfastness for what remains of a life. The request is therefore not a doubter's prayer. It is the prayer of a man who knows how easily a steady heart can slip.

**WHAT THE ASKING TEACHES ABOUT GUIDANCE**

The verse teaches something about guidance itself. Since a person who has been guided still asks to be kept, guidance is not a possession that can be put in a pocket and forgotten. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the point out and ties it to the report that no heart stands firm by itself, since the hearts are turned by the hand of God as He wills. Muslim carries the report. That is why the request is made at all, and that is why it is made by the people with the most knowledge rather than the least.

Ibn ʿUthaymīn notes that the supplication begins with the name of the Lord, and that this is the way the Book usually opens a request, since asking belongs to the care a lord gives his charge. In other words, the words of the prayer teach the manners of prayer. The believer does not come to God as a stranger claiming a right. He comes as a servant whose case is already in the hands of the One he addresses. The difference between a demand and a prayer sits in that word.

Al-Ālūsī records the view that the sentence may also be read as an instruction to the community, so that every reader is taught to make the same request in the same words. Since the verse is placed directly after the account of the well-grounded, the reader is being shown what their knowledge produced in practice. The lesson is not stored in a library. It comes out of the mouth as a prayer, and the community is invited to pray it in turn.

**THE NAME THAT ENDS THE PRAYER**

The prayer closes with ***“indeed the Giver ˹of all bounties˺”***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the name as wide enough to cover every good gift, and al-Jalālayn keeps it short and plain. Al-Baghawī ties the name to the request for mercy by noting that the One asked is the One who gives without being asked twice. A giver who gives a single gift and closes his hand is not what the title carries. The title carries a hand that stays open, and that is why the prayer can end there without further argument.

Think of a man who is given a lamp for a long road. If he asks the giver for oil as well, the request does not insult the gift. It honours the giver, because it treats him as the one who supplies the road. That is the shape of this verse. The guidance already received becomes the reason to ask for more care rather than the reason to stop asking. The believer who understands the gift asks for the strength to keep it.

What the verse leaves the reader with is a habit. A person who fears a change in himself prays before the change arrives, and the people described in the verse are the ones who did exactly that. Since the well-grounded are those who saw the danger in a heart that drifts, the prayer is a proof of their knowledge rather than a doubt about it. The one who thinks he cannot slip has already begun to.

---

## Verse 3:9

> Our Lord! You will certainly gather all humanity for the ˹promised˺ Day—about which there is no doubt. Surely Allah does not break His promise.”

**THE DAY THEY ASK TO BE READY FOR**

The prayer that began with a heart continues toward the Day of Gathering. The verse opens ***“Our Lord! You will certainly gather all humanity”*** and continues ***“for the ˹promised˺ Day”***, and the two lines name the event that gives the whole chapter its weight. Al-Qurṭubī explains the gathering as the raising of people and their restoration to life after they were scattered and buried. Since the words stand inside the prayer of the well-grounded, the resurrection is named there as the reason their request for mercy matters.

Ibn ʿAbbās fills the day with its stations: resurrection after death, the reckoning, the bridge, the scale, and the two abodes on the far side of all of them. Al-Baghawī notes the wording of the appointment, since a promise with a fixed time is not an intention that can drift. The day is named in the verse as something already decided, and the decision is what turns it from a possibility into an appointment. A man who lives as though the appointment might not come lives under a false assumption, and the verse removes it.

**A PROMISE THAT WILL NOT BE BROKEN**

The verse then says ***“about which there is no doubt. Surely Allah does not break His promise.””*** Al-Jalālayn reads the last clause as the answer to the doubt the earlier verses described, and he points out the shift in the sentence from addressing God to speaking about Him. Since the shift happens inside a prayer, the reader is being taught that the matter is settled and finished. What follows from that is a confidence the believers carry into every loss they suffer in the world.

These words complete the well-grounded's speech, and al-Saʿdī reads them as carrying three things at once: an acknowledgement of the resurrection, an acknowledgement of the reckoning, and a certainty that God will carry out everything He promised. He then draws the practical inference, which is that faith in the gathering is the basis of a heart's repair, because desire for good and fear of evil both grow from it. That is why a community under pressure is told about the gathering so often. The Book keeps the last day in view because the present day is where the choices are made.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the people described in the previous verse were praised for asking for the hereafter rather than for comfort, and this verse shows the shape of what they asked to be ready for. Ibn ʿUthaymīn keeps the same line, and al-Ālūsī records the older point that the well-grounded are the people whose knowledge settled this matter in their hearts. Knowledge that does not reach the day of gathering has not finished its work, since the Book's arguments about God end at a life that will be accounted for.

Think of a contract signed and stamped, with the day of settlement written into it. A man may forget the date, but the date does not forget him. The verse treats the gathering that way: the appointment is real, the day is fixed, and the doubt is only in the hearts of the people who live without it. That is the ground the well-grounded stand on when they ask not for wealth or long life, but to be kept steady until they arrive. Since the promise cannot fail, the only open question is how a person will meet it, and the prayer of the verse is one way of answering that question while there is still time.

---

## Verse 3:10

> Indeed, neither the wealth nor children of the disbelievers will be of any benefit to them against Allah—and they will be the fuel for the Fire

**WHAT WEALTH AND CHILDREN CANNOT DO**

The sūrah now names what the people who reject the message trust in, and it takes that trust apart. The verse begins ***“Indeed, neither the wealth nor children of the disbelievers will be of any benefit to them against Allah”***, and the meaning is plain in al-Jalālayn: riches and sons will not protect a man against God or turn aside His punishment. Ibn ʿAbbās names the first audience, since the words were aimed at wealth and standing that the leaders of the city relied on. The verse does not say their money was worthless in this life. The money cannot walk into the account and answer for them.

What the failure covers is worked out by al-Qurṭubī. Wealth and children will not push away the torment by any amount, and no payment will reduce the reckoning. Since the sentence is placed after the account of the gathering, the timing is the point: the goods a person trusted here are things he will not carry there. A man may hold a fortune and an army of sons in the same hand, and both are left at the door of the day that decides his case.

**THE FUEL OF THE FIRE**

The sentence then turns to what the disbelievers themselves become: ***“and they will be the fuel”*** ***“for the Fire”***. Ibn Kathīr explains the image directly, since the people who rejected the message are described as material the fire consumes. Al-Jalālayn records the reading of the word for fuel, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the plain sense of firewood. The picture is exact and grim. What they own cannot save them, and what they are becomes the material of the punishment.

Ibn Kathīr joins this verse to the warning in the next one, where the fate of the people of Pharaoh is named as the pattern for everyone who rejected God's signs before them, and he adds a verse from elsewhere in the Book in which excuses will not help the wrongdoers (40:52). The pairing teaches something about the way God's punishments work in this world and in the next: the earlier nations were seized in their own lifetimes, and the fire waits for the rest. Since the same account is running for every generation, the reader is meant to take the warning personally rather than as history.

Al-Saʿdī keeps the two halves together, since the verse describes what will not help and what will remain. A person may gather money for years to protect himself from every danger he can imagine, and the one danger he cannot buy off is the one that stands after death. Wealth protects a man against hunger, illness and the schemes of his rivals, which means it protects him against the wrong enemies. The enemy left standing is the one standing past death.

**WHY THE WARNING COMES HERE**

The order of the chapter explains the placement. The verses before have been answering a delegation and settling the question of who God is, and the verses after will speak about those who rejected the signs and the believers who lost a battle. Between the two stands this warning, and it is addressed to the confident. The leaders who opposed the message in Madīnah were not the poor of the city. They were men with property, sons and allies, and their strength had convinced them that they could hold their position.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that these two verses serve as the introduction to the warning that follows, in which the believers are told that they will overcome their opponents. The connection is uncomfortable for those opponents and comforting for the believers: the side with money and sons is not automatically the side that wins. Think of a merchant who loads his ship with goods and laughs at the small boat beside him. The sea does not weigh cargo. The fire does not weigh cargo either, and the man who fills his hold with the wrong things has made himself part of the load.

That is what turns on the last words of the verse. The disbelievers are not merely the people who miss a reward. They become the material of the punishment they were warned about, which means the warning is not a threat held over their heads from outside. They walk into it with their own hands, holding the wealth that could have been given in the path they denied. The reader is left with a question about what he is gathering, and about which fire he is feeding.

---

## Verse 3:11

> Their fate will be like that of the people of Pharaoh and those before them—they all rejected Our signs, so Allah seized them for their sins. And Allah is severe in punishment

**A PATTERN THAT REACHES BACK THROUGH HISTORY**

The warning to the confident is followed by a comparison drawn from the old nations: ***“Their fate will be like”*** and ***“that of the people of Pharaoh”***. Al-Ṭabarī reads the comparison as the answer to the confidence of the previous verse, since the wealth and children of the disbelievers will not help them when the punishment arrives, exactly as they did not help the households that came before. Al-Qurṭubī notes the old sense of the phrase, which carries the idea of a settled habit, a course a people keeps to until it is their ruin. The nations in the verse were not surprised by an unfamiliar disaster. They walked a road that had already been walked.

The next words widen the comparison beyond Egypt: ***“and those before them”***. Al-Jalālayn names the nations that fit the description, and the point is that the pattern is not tied to one place or one tyrant. Since every generation that refused the signs met the same end, the verse turns a piece of history into a rule. What follows for the reader is that the confidence in the previous verse is not a new danger. It is the oldest one in the record.

**WHAT THEY DID AND WHAT FOLLOWED**

The reason for the ruin is stated plainly: ***“they all rejected Our signs”***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the rejection as the refusal of the Book and the messenger, and he adds that the first audience of the verse had done to the Prophet ﷺ what the people of Mūsā had done to him before. They gave him the lie and abused him. That is why the verse can speak about Pharaoh in the middle of an address to the people of Madīnah, since the same conduct had reappeared in a new city.

The consequence is named in the same order: ***“so Allah seized them”*** and ***“for their sins. And Allah is severe in punishment”***. Al-Saʿdī draws out the two levels of the seizing, since the punishment came on some of them in their own world and the rest waits for the gathering. That is why the verse closes with severity rather than with a date. A punishment that arrives late is still a punishment, and the record of the old nations is kept in the Book so that the later reader can measure his own road against theirs.

Ibn ʿAbbās returns to the connection with Badr at the end of his account. The people who had abused the messenger in Madīnah would meet what the household of Pharaoh met, and the day of Badr was the beginning of that answer. The verse does not leave the warning in the past, since the pattern is written into the way God deals with nations, and what happened at the sea was repeated at the wells of Badr. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān treats the two verses as the introduction to the warning that follows, and the order teaches the reader that the invitation to learn from history comes before the prediction of what the living will meet.

Think of a man who builds his house on a bank that has washed away twice within living memory. He does not need a new argument when the water rises again. He needs to move the house. The oldest floods in the record make that point, and the decision is left with the man on the bank. A nation that reads the line and builds anyway has chosen the water, and the last words of the verse describe what waits at the end of the choice.

---

## Verse 3:12

> ˹O Prophet!˺ Tell the disbelievers, “Soon you will be overpowered and driven to Hell—what an evil place to rest!”

**A DECLARATION MADE TO A CONFIDENT CITY**

The warning becomes a command to speak: ***“˹O Prophet!˺ Tell the disbelievers, “Soon you will be overpowered”*** and ***“and driven to Hell”***. The promise of defeat is placed in the mouth of the messenger, which means the outcome is announced before the fighting begins. Ibn Kathīr explains that the overpowering belongs to this life and the gathering to the next, so the sentence carries two defeats in one. What follows for the community is confidence in a promise spoken in advance, and what follows for the disbelievers is a warning that could still have been answered while there was time.

The occasion is kept by al-Qurṭubī in a report from Muḥammad b. Isḥāq. After the news of Badr reached Madīnah, the Jews of the city were gathered and warned that what had come on Quraysh could come on them. Their answer is preserved by al-Jalālayn: they told him not to fool himself because he had killed a few men of Quraysh who knew nothing about fighting. Since the answer treated the victory as an accident, the verse was sent down to treat it as a beginning. The same report is carried by al-Ālūsī on the authority of Abū Ṣāliḥ from Ibn ʿAbbās, with the leaders of the city agreeing among themselves that the man was the prophet their own books had promised.

**THE WARNING AND WHO IT ADDRESSED**

The disbelievers are named here without limiting the address to one nation, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān explains that the ones meant are the disbelievers of that time in particular. That limitation matters, since the promise of defeat was not a rule that every unbeliever in every age falls in battle. It was a statement about the people who had heard the message and chosen to fight it, and the statement came true in their own lifetimes. A prediction that names your own city and is fulfilled in your own street is not a general hope. It is an account that has to be answered.

Ibn ʿAbbās gives the two halves of the outcome in plain words: they would be overcome at Badr and gathered to the fire on the day of judgement. Al-Ṭabarī records the readings of the sentence, since the form may be heard as addressed to the disbelievers or as reporting about them, and the difference is a matter of who is being told. What turns on it is small in sense and useful in warning: either the disbelievers are addressed directly, or the believers are told what will happen to them. In both readings the result is settled.

**AN EVIL PLACE TO REST**

The sentence ends with ***“what an evil place to rest!””***. The wording pictures a resting place, which makes the description sharper, since a place of rest is what a tired person hopes for at the end of a journey. Al-Saʿdī treats the whole verse as a promise to the believers and a threat to the disbelievers, and he notes that the announcement was fulfilled in a way that had no equal in the history of the Arabs. Because the end of the road is named before the road is walked, the reader learns where the two paths lead while he still stands at the fork.

Al-Qurṭubī keeps the report of the gathering and the warning in the order they happened, since the invitation came first and the refusal came after it. A man who is told where his road ends and keeps walking has made his choice twice: once when he refused the message, and once when he heard the end of the road and kept to it. The verse closes with the picture of a rest that no one would choose, and the reader is left to decide which way he is travelling.

---

## Verse 3:13

> Indeed, there was a sign for you in the two armies that met in battle—one fighting for the cause of Allah and the other in denial. The believers saw their enemy twice their number. But Allah supports with His victory whoever He wills. Surely in this is a lesson for people of insight

**THE SIGN THAT WAS SEEN AT BADR**

The promise of defeat is followed by evidence already in the hands of its hearers: ***“Indeed, there was a sign”*** and ***“for you in the two armies”*** and ***“that met in battle”***. Al-Ṭabarī explains the sign as the proof that the promise about the disbelievers was true, since the battle had already taken place when the verse was heard. The argument is therefore built on an event the audience could check, and that is why the verse can speak about the past to establish what is coming.

The two sides are named in the verse: ***“one fighting for the cause of Allah”*** and ***“and the other in denial. The believers saw their enemy twice their number.”***. Ibn ʿAbbās gives the numbers of the day, with the believers a small company and the enemy around a thousand, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the same account. The smallness of the believing band is the point of the sign, since a victory that needs no help proves nothing. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws out the lesson for the community that had just fought and would fight again: the numbers were never the deciding factor, and the same hand that decided Badr decides what comes after it.

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Baghawī record the older discussion about the wording of the sentence, since the verb of being is masculine where the word for sign is grammatically feminine. The explanations differ and the conclusion is small: the phrase was joined to the idea of a clear showing rather than to the word itself. Since the audience of the verse was Arab and the argument was about meaning rather than grammar, the discussion belongs to the study of the Book rather than to its claim.

**THE HAND THAT DECIDES**

The verse then fixes the cause of the outcome: ***“But Allah supports with His victory whoever He wills. Surely in this is a lesson”***. The choosing is named as God's, and that keeps the believers from reading their victory as a statement about their own strength. Al-Saʿdī puts it plainly, since the victory at Badr became a sign of the truth of the messenger and of the falsehood of his enemies. A battle is decided in the field, and its meaning is decided by what the Book says about it. What follows is that a small force can win and still be taught humility, because the win was granted rather than earned.

Ibn Kathīr reads the last phrase as a lesson for people who understand, and the phrase is placed at the end of the verse as a condition. Since the sign sits in plain history, the difference between those who learn from it and those who do not is not intelligence but attention. Think of two merchants who read the same shipping news: one sells his goods before the storm, and the other buys more. The information is the same, and the outcome separates them.

The verse closes with ***“for people of insight”***, and the phrase is a gate rather than a compliment. Ibn ʿAbbās describes such people as those who weigh what they see, and the verse gives them the battle as material. The community had seen two armies meet and had seen which one won. The question the verse leaves is whether the reader will treat the event as an accident of war or as a sign from the Lord of the worlds, and the answer decides what he does when his own small company meets its own large one.

---

## Verse 3:14

> The enjoyment of ˹worldly˺ desires—women, children, treasures of gold and silver, fine horses, cattle, and fertile land—has been made appealing to people. These are the pleasures of this worldly life, but with Allah is the finest destination

**WHAT PEOPLE FIND APPEALING**

The sūrah now opens a window on the human heart, and the list is long: ***“The enjoyment of ˹worldly˺ desires”*** and then ***“women, children, treasures of gold”*** and ***“and silver, fine horses, cattle”*** and ***“and fertile land”***. Ibn ʿAbbās explains the first items as the strongest pulls on a man: the love of a wife, the hope invested in sons, and the wealth that secures both. Al-Jalālayn keeps the list in its plain sense, and al-Qurṭubī notes that the order itself is a lesson, since the desires are arranged in the order in which they take hold of a person.

The way the sentence is built draws attention from al-Saʿdī, since the appetite for these things was planted in people rather than taught to them. That is why the verse does not begin with a prohibition. It begins with a description, and the description is the reason the command that comes later will need patience. A farmer who knows that weeds grow without being sown does not stand in the field and scold the weeds. He plants, and he returns to clear them.

**THE SIZE OF WHAT IS LISTED**

The list is closed with ***“has been made appealing to people. These are the pleasures of this worldly life”***, and the description is the point of the sentence. Ibn Kathīr reads the phrase as a verdict on the whole catalogue, since the goods named are provisions of travel rather than the destination itself. Al-Ālūsī keeps the same line, treating them as adornments that belong to this world and pass with it. A man may hold every item on the list and still be holding only the travel bag.

The items are worked through one by one by al-Qurṭubī, and his treatment shows that the Book is not embarrassed by wealth. Horses, cattle and land are the means by which a city feeds itself and defends itself. The verse does not call them evil, since the term used is enjoyment and pleasure rather than filth or harm. What it does is put them at their true size, and the size is measured by the last words of the sentence. The same point is put in plain terms by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān: the verse is not an attack on the world. It is a warning against the world being treated as the whole of the account.

**THE FINEST DESTINATION**

The sentence turns at the end: ***“but with Allah is the finest destination”***. The contrast is not between something good and something bad. It is between a good thing and a better one, which is why Ibn Kathīr notes that the last word describes a return rather than an upgrade. A merchant who is offered a fine stall in a good market is not wrong to take it. He is wrong if he takes it and forgets that he is on the road home.

Think of a market stall lit for one evening of the year. The lights are real, the goods are real, and the crowd is happy. The man who sits in the stall until dawn will find the market empty and the fires out. This life is treated here the way that man should have treated the evening, and the destination is named so that the road keeps its meaning. Since the finest thing is not on the list, the list can be enjoyed without being trusted, and that is the freedom the verse offers to the reader.

---

## Verse 3:15

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Shall I inform you of what is better than ˹all of˺ this? Those mindful ˹of Allah˺ will have Gardens with their Lord under which rivers flow, to stay there forever, and pure spouses, along with Allah’s pleasure.” And Allah is All-Seeing of ˹His˺ servants

**A QUESTION THAT INVITES THE ANSWER**

The comparison raised in the previous verse is answered with a question put in the messenger's mouth: ***“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Shall I inform you of what is better than ˹all of˺ this?”***. The question invites the answer rather than forcing it, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that this is the way the Book treats people who are attached to what they hold. By asking whether they want to hear about something better, the sentence makes the listener choose to hear, and the choice is part of the teaching. Al-Jalālayn keeps the sense of the question and the answer that follows it.

The answer is given to the people described as mindful of God: ***“Those mindful ˹of Allah˺ will have Gardens with their Lord under”*** and ***“which rivers flow, to stay there forever”***. Al-Baghawī explains the rivers as the well-known feature of the gardens promised in the Book, and Ibn Kathīr notes that the permanence is part of what makes the gift better than the goods of the world. The gardens do not pass and the guests do not leave. What turns on that difference is the whole lesson of the passage, since every pleasure on the earlier list comes with a clock attached to it.

**THE SPOUSES AND THE PLEASURE THAT STANDS ABOVE THEM**

The answer continues with ***“and pure spouses, along with Allah’s pleasure.””***. Al-Qurṭubī explains the purity as freedom from everything that spoils a companion in this world: no weariness, no illness, no failing of temper, and no end to the union. Since the first item on the list of desires was the love between men and women, the answer returns to it and gives it in a form that lasts. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same sense and treats the phrase as the best of what is promised to the people of the Garden.

Then the sentence names what stands above all of it: the pleasure of God. Ibn Kathīr and al-Saʿdī both give this the highest place, since a servant may have every gift and still be separated from what he most wants, which is to know that his Lord is pleased with him. The pleasure named here is not a thing a person can purchase, and that is why the verse puts it beside the gardens rather than among their trees. The greatest of the gifts is not inside the garden. It is the welcome of the One who made it.

**ALL-SEEING OF HIS SERVANTS**

The verse ends with ***“And Allah is All-Seeing of ˹His˺ servants”***. The name is placed there to answer a quiet assumption, since the reader who hears about gardens for the mindful may wonder whether the difference will really be noticed. Al-Qurṭubī ties the closing words to the verses that follow, where the people of the Garden are shown praying for forgiveness and asking to be kept from the fire. Because God sees His servants, the small deeds are not lost, and the hidden struggle of a believing heart is not invisible.

Think of a craftsman who keeps a record of every hand that helped him on a difficult job. The help may have been a small thing done quietly on a hot day, and the craftsman remembers the man who gave it. The same is said of the One who made the hands. A servant who gives in secret, prays before dawn, or holds his tongue when he could answer back is seen, and the destination named in the verse is prepared for people who are seen in exactly that way. That is the ground of the invitation, and it is why the passage ends with sight rather than with wealth.

---

## Verse 3:16

> who pray, “Our Lord! We have believed, so forgive our sins and protect us from the torment of the Fire.”

**THE PRAYER THAT DESCRIBES THEM**

The people promised the gardens are described here by what they say, and the description begins ***“who pray, “Our Lord!”*** and continues ***“We have believed, so forgive our sins”*** and ***“and protect us from the torment of the Fire.””***. The supplication is part of their identity rather than an occasional act, which means the verse is drawing a portrait and not listing a duty. Al-Ṭabarī joins the sentence to the promise before it, since the question asked in the previous verse is answered by the description of the people who receive it.

Ibn ʿAbbās explains the words of the prayer as belief in God and in His messenger, and he notes the breadth of the request for forgiveness, since it covers what a person did before guidance and what he does after it. The same sense is kept in a short line by al-Jalālayn. Since a believer's record is not closed by his first act of faith, the prayer is not an insult to his belief. It is the natural speech of a man who knows that his account is still running.

**WHAT THE PRAYER ASKS FOR AND WHY**

The request has two halves, and al-Qurṭubī treats them in order: forgiveness for what has passed, and protection from what is coming. The first half looks back and the second looks forward, which means the prayer covers the whole of a life rather than one moment in it. Al-Saʿdī draws out the manner of the asking, since these people approach their Lord through the faith He gave them, and that is one of the ways of asking that God loves. A servant who says he believes and then asks for forgiveness is not bargaining. He is asking for the gift to be completed.

Ibn Kathīr notes that the fear named in the second half is fear of the fire rather than fear of poverty or illness, and he treats that as a mark of the people described. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān makes the same point from the side of knowledge, since the request shows what these people considered worth asking for. A man's prayers reveal his measure. The one who asks only for the world has his eyes on the road, and the one who asks to be kept from the fire has his eyes on the end of it.

Think of a man who has crossed a wide river safely and then asks the ferryman about the road on the far side. The crossing was real, and the request is not a doubt about it. It is a sign that the journey continues. The verse places that request in the mouths of the people promised the gardens, which means the best of the community are still asking. Since the promise is certain and the road is long, the two facts fit together without strain.

**WHERE THE DESCRIPTION LEADS**

The prayer stands between the promise and the list that follows, and the position is deliberate. The next verses will describe the same people by their patience, their truthfulness, their spending, and their asking at dawn. What moves through all of it is a heart that does not trust itself, and the prayer is the clearest expression of that. Al-Qurṭubī connects the two passages for exactly this reason, since the prayer is the root of the qualities.

The verse also answers a question a reader may carry from the gardens: who are the people who receive them? The answer is not a title or a lineage. It is a group of people who admit their need in the plainest words, and the words are preserved in the Book so that every later believer can make them his own. (3:135 — **“remember Allah and seek forgiveness”**) belongs to the same pattern, where the people who slip are taught to come back rather than to run. What follows from that is a door that stays open, and the verse leaves the reader standing in it.

---

## Verse 3:17

> ˹It is they˺ who are patient, sincere, obedient, and charitable, and who pray for forgiveness before dawn

**THE QUALITIES THAT MARK THEM**

The portrait continues with four qualities and a habit. The verse opens ***“˹It is they˺ who are patient, sincere, obedient”*** and adds ***“and charitable, and who pray”*** and ***“for forgiveness before dawn”***. Al-Ṭabarī explains the patience as endurance in hardship and in battle, the sincerity as truthfulness with God in word and deed, the obedience as a settled devotion, and the spending as what a person gives out of what he owns. Each quality is small enough to practise and heavy enough to test a life.

The four qualities are placed together because each one needs the others. Al-Jalālayn explains the patience as endurance in obedience and resistance to disobedience, which means patience here is more than the quiet bearing of a loss. Since patience in this sense is work rather than waiting, a person cannot claim it by sitting still. Al-Baghawī keeps the same reading and adds that truthfulness is measured by the fulfilment of what a person has promised God.

**THE HOUR BEFORE DAWN**

The verse closes with a habit rather than a feeling: ***“for forgiveness before dawn”***. Al-Jalālayn notes that the hour was singled out because it is a time when people are unaware of one another and sleep is at its sweetest, which makes the asking there a proof of what a person really wants. A man who gets up before the light to ask forgiveness has decided in the dark what matters to him. Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Kathīr keep the same line, and the reports join the practice to the night prayer that the early community kept.

Al-Ālūsī treats the endurance named here as patience in obedience and against disobedience, and he arranges the qualities in the order in which they grow in a person: a truthful man is patient, a patient man obeys, and an obedient man spends. In other words, the list is not four separate medals. It is one character seen from four sides. (98:5 — **“with sincere devotion to Him”**) uses the same standard for the people of the earlier books, which means the qualities are not a new invention for a new community.

Think of a farmer who checks the state of his fields in the last quiet hour before the day begins. That hour is when the work of the previous days is understood and the work of the coming day is planned. The verse takes that hour and gives it to prayer, since the account of a soul is examined best when no one is watching. Ibn ʿUthaymīn notes that the asking at dawn is placed after the spending in the list, and the order teaches something about how a person's religion travels from his wealth to his heart.

What the verse leaves the reader with is a standard that can be measured. Patience, truthfulness, obedience, giving, and a quiet hour before dawn are things a person can check in himself without being told. Since the people promised the gardens are described in these terms, the description is also a road. The qualities can be built, and the verse places the building inside an ordinary day, where a man's hours and money and words are his material. That is what the picture of the gardens costs, and the Book names the price plainly so that no reader can say it was hidden from him.

---

## Verse 3:18

> Allah ˹Himself˺ is a Witness that there is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him—and so are the angels and people of knowledge. He is the Maintainer of justice. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him—the Almighty, All-Wise

**THE GREATEST WITNESS ON THE GREATEST MATTER**

The chapter now returns to its central claim, and it brings witnesses: ***“Allah ˹Himself˺ is a Witness”*** and ***“that there is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him”***. Al-Saʿdī calls this the most weighty of all testimonies, given by the King, His angels, and the people of knowledge about the most weighty thing a witness can address. Since the oneness of God is the root of the law and the root of the account that follows it, the verse places the whole religion on a foundation that its own Creator testifies to.

Al-Jalālayn explains the witness as God making the matter clear to His creation through proofs and signs, which means the testimony runs through the world itself rather than remaining a sentence in a book. Every created thing that points to a maker is part of the showing, and that showing is God's own witness. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the plain sense and adds that the angels and the learned also bear the witness, so that the claim arrives with company.

**WHY THE ANGELS AND THE LEARNED ARE NAMED**

The verse continues ***“and so are the angels”*** and ***“and people of knowledge. He is the Maintainer of justice. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him”***. Ibn ʿAbbās identifies the people of knowledge as the prophets and the believers, and al-Saʿdī notes that knowledge is the quality that makes a witness worth hearing. Since the angels witness by their nature and the learned witness by their study, the two kinds of testimony cover the heavens and the earth. What follows is that ignorance is not a neutral position in this matter. It is a missing witness.

Al-Qurṭubī carries the report of Saʿīd b. Jubayr that three hundred and sixty idols stood around the Kaʿbah, and that when this verse came down they fell prostrate. The report is a picture of the claim's power in the place where its opposite was worshipped. He also records, on al-Kalbī's authority, that two rabbis from Syria came to Madīnah and recognised the city from the description in their books, and that the verse was given in connection with them. Al-Baghawī ties the verse to the Christians of Najran, since the sūrah has been answering them from its opening.

**THE MAINTAINER OF JUSTICE**

The words ***“He is the Maintainer of justice”*** are placed inside the testimony, and they tie belief to conduct. Since the One who testifies to His own oneness also maintains justice, the God described here is not a distant owner but a ruler whose rule is measured. Al-Ālūsī takes up the grammar of the sentence and the weight of the phrase, and the conclusion is that the justice belongs to the standing of the One who holds it. A judge who is just must be able to see the case, and the One named here sees it.

The sentence then repeats the declaration and closes with the two names: ***“the Almighty, All-Wise”***. Al-Ṭabarī reads the repetition as emphasis, since a matter of this weight is stated twice so that the reader cannot pass over it. The power and the wisdom answer two questions a person carries: whether God can act on what He knows, and whether He knows what He is doing. Think of a court where the judge who hears the case is also the one who built the city and wrote its law. The verse puts the reader in front of that bench and names the witnesses, and it leaves him with the only question that remains, which is what he will do with the testimony he has now heard.

---

## Verse 3:19

> Certainly, Allah’s only Way is Islam. Those who were given the Scripture did not dispute ˹among themselves˺ out of mutual envy until knowledge came to them. Whoever denies Allah’s signs, then surely Allah is swift in reckoning

**THE WAY THAT GOD ACCEPTS**

The religion that stands with God is named at the start: ***“Certainly, Allah’s only Way is Islam. Those”***. Al-Ṭabarī explains the religion here as obedience and submission, which means the verse is drawing a posture rather than a label. Al-Qurṭubī keeps that reading and notes that the submission spoken of includes belief and the acts that follow it. Since the sense is the handing over of oneself, the claim of the verse is that God accepts nothing less and nothing other than this.

Al-Saʿdī draws the sense out plainly, since the religion with God is the one for which no other is accepted, and it is the yielding to God alone, outward and inward, in the way His messengers brought. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān traces this sense through the language and shows that the earlier prophets were sent with the same footing. What follows is that the verse is not announcing a new religion. It is naming the religion that was always the one accepted, and the difference between the messengers is in the law they carried rather than in the submission asked of them.

**THE DISPUTE THAT FOLLOWED KNOWLEDGE**

The verse then addresses the communities that argued: ***“who were given the Scripture did not dispute ˹among themselves˺ out of mutual envy”*** and ***“until knowledge came to them. Whoever denies Allah’s signs”***. The order of the sentence matters, since the dispute came after knowledge rather than before it. Al-Jalālayn explains the difference as rivalry over position, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the same sense: the argument was not born of ignorance, and that is why it is a fault rather than a mistake.

Ibn ʿAbbās records the occasion in a short report, since two men from the Syrian lands came asking which witness in the Book of God was the greatest, and when the answer was given they accepted Islam. The report sits well with the verse before it, where the testimony was named. Since the communities knew what the Book said about the oneness of God, their dispute was about the standing that came with a claim, and the verse's verdict falls on the motive rather than on the words. That is why the fault is named as envy rather than as error.

**A RECKONING THAT DOES NOT WAIT**

The sentence closes with ***“then surely Allah is swift in reckoning”***. Al-Saʿdī notes that the swiftness belongs to God's accounting, so that no one supposes the dispute passes unrecorded because it passes unpunished in this world. A warning about the punishment of the fire was given in an earlier passage, and here the warning takes the form of speed. A reckoning that does not need to wait for paperwork is a reckoning that can arrive at any hour.

Think of a shopkeeper who changes the labels on his goods and counts on the customers not noticing until he has left the street. The inspection is not on a schedule the deceiver controls. Since the sign of God was already given, the one who denies it is not waiting for information. He is waiting for the day when the answer will be given, and the last words of the verse describe that day as near. What follows for the reader is a decision about what to do with what he has already heard, and the verse makes that decision the whole of the matter.

---

## Verse 3:20

> So if they argue with you ˹O Prophet˺, say, “I have submitted myself to Allah, and so have my followers.” And ask those who were given the Scripture and the illiterate ˹people˺, “Have you submitted yourselves ˹to Allah˺?” If they submit, they will be ˹rightly˺ guided. But if they turn away, then your duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. And Allah is All-Seeing of ˹His˺ servants

**THE ANSWER GIVEN TO AN ARGUMENT**

The argument described in the previous verse is answered with a declaration rather than a rebuttal: ***“So if they argue with you ˹O Prophet˺, say, “I have submitted myself to Allah”*** and ***“and so have my followers.””***. The disputants here, as al-Ṭabarī notes, are the ones who argued about ʿĪsā, and the answer given to the messenger is to state his own position rather than to trade points. A man who has handed himself over does not need to win the debate in order to hold his ground.

Al-Jalālayn explains the choice of wording here, since the surrender is expressed through the face, the most honoured part of a person, with the sense that the rest of the body follows where the face has led. Al-Qurṭubī keeps the same reading and notes that the command is to rest the matter on what the messenger was given to carry. In other words, the reply turns the discussion away from the disputants and back to the duty, which is a pattern the sūrah has followed since its opening.

**THE QUESTION PUT TO BOTH COMMUNITIES**
