They had come to claim a rank for ʿĪsā on the basis of his mother's honour, and the sūrah grants the honour in full and then places it where it belongs. The chosen woman served in a sanctuary, prayed with the worshippers, and received her provision from God. That is the house from which her son came, and the sūrah is about to say so in plain words. The reader is left with a portrait of a servant rather than a personage, and the portrait is what the Book honours.

---

## Verse 3:43

> O Mary! Be devout to your Lord, prostrate yourself ˹in prayer˺ and bow along with those who bow down.”

**THE COMMAND THAT FOLLOWED THE HONOUR**

The announcement of the choice is followed by an instruction: ***“O Mary! Be devout to your Lord, prostrate yourself ˹in prayer˺”*** and ***“and bow along with those”*** and ***“who bow down.””***. Al-Ṭabarī explains the first command as a call to give her obedience to her Lord alone and to keep it devoted to Him. The order of the two sentences is the lesson: the honour was announced, and the next thing she heard was what the honour asked of her. A rank that comes from God does not remove the duty; it increases it.

Ibn ʿAbbās reads the command as gratitude and adds the older explanation that it means to prolong the prayer, and al-Qurṭubī keeps both readings and notes the reports about the word. The two senses belong together, since a person who has been given a large gift stands longer in prayer than one who has not. The verse gives the girl no festival and no procession. It gives her a posture, and the posture is the one used by everyone who serves in the house.

**THE ORDER OF THE POSTURES AND THE QUALIFIER**

The three acts are named in an order that is worth noticing, since prostration is mentioned before bowing. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān takes up the qualifier that is attached to the bowing and not to the prostration, and observes what it suggests about the way people perform the two acts. Worshippers generally guard their prostration and hurry their bowing, and the verse attaches the bowing to the company of those who perform it fully. The instruction to bow with the bowers is therefore a model to be matched rather than a movement to be made.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān also notes the reports of how Maryam was given a place among the worshippers, and the sense that comes from the command is that she was told to pray with a congregation rather than in a private corner. Al-Saʿdī keeps the plain sense of the three acts as worship offered in company with the people of the house. A girl raised in the temple and housed in a chamber is told to stand with the people at prayer. Since the honour named in the previous verse was hers alone, the duty named here is common to everyone, and the verse joins the two.

**THE LESSON OF THE LITTLE VERSE**

The verse is short, and every part of it is a command, which makes it a summary of how the sūrah treats honour. The chosen woman is told to be devout, to prostrate, and to bow with the bowers. There is no instruction about her lineage, no mention of a privilege, and no request for anything for herself. What follows for the reader is a standard that can be practised in any mosque in any city.

Think of a man who is promoted in a company and finds on his desk, next to the notice of the promotion, a list of the hours he will now be expected to keep. The two documents belong together, and the verse gives them in the same breath. The honour announced to Maryam is followed by the work that the honour carried, and the Book records the work as part of the honour. The next verse turns from the girl to the reader of the story, and it explains how such news reaches a man who was not there.

---

## Verse 3:44

> This is news of the unseen that We reveal to you ˹O Prophet˺. You were not with them when they cast lots to decide who would be Mary’s guardian, nor were you there when they argued ˹about it˺

**THE STORY THAT NO ONE COULD HAVE CARRIED**

The narrative pauses to speak about its own arrival: ***“This is news of the unseen that We reveal to you ˹O Prophet˺.”***. Al-Ṭabarī gathers the passages under the word this, since the stories of the wife of ʿImrān, of her daughter, of Zachariah and of his son are all included in one sentence, and he reads the phrase as the statement that these accounts came from God rather than from a source in Madīnah. Al-Qurṭubī notes what the sentence proves, since a man in Arabia was told the details of a family in Jerusalem four centuries of story earlier.

The argument the passage makes for the prophethood of Muḥammad ﷺ is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān. The events of the sanctuary were not recorded in the hands of his people, and he had no teachers among the priests of the house. The knowledge therefore came from the One who knows what is hidden, and the sentence states it in plain words. A reader who accepts the account has accepted the messenger who carried it.

**THE LOT AND THE QUARREL**

The sentence then names two scenes that the Prophet ﷺ did not witness. The first is ***“You were not with them when they cast lots to decide who would be Mary's guardian”***, and the second is ***“nor were you there when they argued ˹about it˺”***. Ibn ʿAbbās describes the casting as the throwing of pens into the water and the quarrel as the dispute of each guardian over his better claim. Al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense and notes that the details of the scene could only have reached the Prophet ﷺ by revelation. The verse does not say the story was widely known. The messenger was not present at the events that produced the account.

Ibn Kathīr keeps the two elements together, and the pairing is instructive: one is a decision made by a lot and the other is an argument about honour. The Book preserves both, including the part that reflects no credit on anyone, and that is a mark of the account's honesty. A story invented for a purpose tends to leave out the quarrelling. The verse records it, and then leaves the argument where it ended, with the lot.

**WHY THE VERSE STANDS WHERE IT DOES**

The verse is placed in the middle of the family story rather than at its end, and al-Qurṭubī explains the position by noting that the passage is an answer to people who would question the source of the account. Once the messenger has been told what he could not have learned, the story can proceed to the birth of ʿĪsā, which is the central claim at issue with the delegation from Najran. The verse is a hinge, and the next sentences swing on it.

Think of a witness in a court who is asked how he knows what happened in a closed room. If his account contains a detail that only someone inside the room could have known, the question about his source is answered by the detail itself. The details of the sanctuary work in exactly that way, and they arrive before the reader reaches the claim that matters most. What follows is a story told from the inside of a room, and the Book says so before it tells it.

---

## Verse 3:45

> ˹Remember˺ when the angels proclaimed, “O Mary! Allah gives you good news of a Word from Him, his name will be the Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary; honoured in this world and the Hereafter, and he will be one of those nearest ˹to Allah˺

**THE NEWS BROUGHT TO THE GIRL**

The announcement returns to the chamber and widens it: ***“˹Remember˺ when the angels proclaimed, “O Mary!”*** and ***“Allah gives you good news of a Word from Him, his name will be the Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary”***. Al-Jalālayn explains what the Word means here, since the child would come into being by a command from God rather than through a father, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same reading. The gift is announced with a name before it is announced with a birth, and the name is the first thing the girl receives.

Ibn ʿAbbās records the older explanations of the title Messiah, including one that takes it from the travelling of the man through the land and another that takes it as a mark of honour. Al-Jalālayn notes the choice to attribute the child to his mother rather than to a father, and the choice is part of the message the child came to carry. The naming in the verse is therefore teaching, and it is placed before the birth so that the community that would argue about him could hear it first.

**THE HONOUR THAT IS SPOKEN IN ADVANCE**

The sentence continues: ***“honoured in this world”*** and ***“and the Hereafter, and he will be one of those nearest ˹to Allah˺”***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the first as the standing and position the man would have among people, and the second as his standing with God. So the announcement gives the child two honours, one that people could see and one that only God could give, and the order places the visible honour first. The delegation from Najran had argued from the honoured place of ʿĪsā among the people, and the verse grants that honour and adds the rest.

Al-Ṭabarī explains the word used for honour as rank and nearness, and the phrase about those nearest answers a question the honour raises. A man may be honoured by his people and still be far from his Lord. The child described here is near, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the same reading. The nearness is the highest of the gifts in the sentence, and it is given to a servant rather than to a partner, which is the whole of the answer the sūrah is building. Al-Saʿdī draws the same line, since a brought-near servant is still a servant.

**WHAT THE ANNOUNCEMENT SETTLES**

The verse settles three things at once, and the reader should notice the order. The child is a Word from God, which explains the manner of his coming. He is the Messiah, ʿĪsā son of Mary, which settles his identity and his family. He is honoured and brought near, which settles his rank. Every one of these points was disputed by somebody, and the Book states them before the birth so that no later argument could say it had been left open.

Think of a family that is told the name and the future of a child before the child arrives, and then watches the announcement come true in detail. The verse is that announcement, and it is placed in the middle of the sūrah's answer to a delegation that came arguing about the man's nature. What follows in the next verses is the way the birth happened and the words the child would speak, and the reader is invited to measure the announcement against the account. The two are kept in the same story, and the story is what the Book asks to be remembered.

---

## Verse 3:46

> And he will speak to people in ˹his˺ infancy and adulthood and will be one of the righteous.”

**THE CHILD WHO WOULD SPEAK**

The announcement adds a sign: ***“And he will speak to people in ˹his˺ infancy”*** and ***“and adulthood and will be one of the righteous.””***. Ibn ʿAbbās explains the first as speech given to a child still in his mother's lap, and he records the words that childhood speech would carry, which were the confession that he was the servant of God and His messenger. Al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense and notes that the speech would come before the age when children speak. The sign is a voice coming from a cradle, and it answers an accusation before the child can be old enough to defend his mother.

The second half names the ordinary course of a life: ***“and adulthood and will be one of the righteous.””***. The manhood is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as the age of thirty and after, when the mission would begin, and Ibn Kathīr keeps the same sequence. The verse therefore gives two speeches, one from a cradle and one from a prophet, and the two together make the point. The man who spoke as a baby would speak again as an adult, and the first speech was the sign that the second would be true.

**WHY THE TWO AGES ARE NAMED TOGETHER**

The pairing of infancy and adulthood answers an objection that the miracle of the cradle could raise on its own. A voice from a cradle can be taken as a wonder of the moment, and the verse joins it to a whole life of speech so that the reader sees one man rather than one marvel. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws out the lesson, since the child who spoke in the cradle would grow to teach, to call, and to warn, and the sign was the opening of a mission rather than a stunt.

The phrase is read by Ibn Kathīr as part of the qualities listed for the child, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes the setting of the passage in the sūrah, since the announcement of virtues here prepares the reader for the account of ʿĪsā's mission and his miracles later in the chapter. Al-Baghawī keeps the two ages in the same order and reads the sentence as a description of the whole span of the mission. The verse gives the man his capacities before it gives him his tasks, and the order keeps the reader from mistaking a wonder for a person.

**THE RIGHTEOUSNESS THAT CLOSES THE LIST**

The sentence ends with ***“and will be one of the righteous.””***, and the last word of the announcement is the plainest of them. Ibn ʿAbbās explains the righteous as the messengers, and the list that began with a name and a title ends with a character. So the announcement moves from the manner of the birth to the rank of the man and finally to the quality he would carry, and the quality is one that the community of Madīnah understood in their own law.

Think of a craftsman who is described to a customer by the tools he will use, the shop he will keep, and the reputation he has kept for twenty years. The last of the three is what makes the first two worth buying. The verse closes the announcement of ʿĪsā in exactly that order, and it does so before the birth. The sūrah has been answering the claim that he shares in divinity, and the announcement answers it by naming the man's servant-hood, his mission and his righteousness, which are the marks of a prophet rather than of a partner.

---

## Verse 3:47

> Mary wondered, “My Lord! How can I have a child when no man has ever touched me?” An angel replied, “So will it be. Allah creates what He wills. When He decrees a matter, He simply tells it, ‘Be!’ And it is

**THE QUESTION OF THE VIRGIN**

The announcement is met with the same kind of question Zachariah had asked: ***“Mary wondered, “My Lord!”*** and ***“How can I have a child”*** and ***“when no man has ever touched me?””***. Al-Qurṭubī notes that she addressed the angel as her master and that the same question is preserved in the sūrah of Maryam, where she adds that she has never been unchaste. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps both halves of the denial, so that the question covers the lawful way and the unlawful one. The girl was not doubting a promise. She was asking how a thing could come to her that had no road to her.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān explains the question as a request about the means rather than a doubt about the power, and the reading matches the shape of the answer that follows. A woman who has spent her life in a sanctuary knows the ordinary course of births, and she is asking whether she is to marry or whether the child will come without the ordinary course. The question is asked by a servant who wants to understand what she has been told, and the verse keeps it as part of the story.

**THE ANSWER THAT NAMES THE CREATOR**

The reply begins ***“An angel replied, “So will it be. Allah creates what He wills.”***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads it as the statement that the child will be created without a father, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense and adds that God creates what He will. The answer does not explain a mechanism. It names a creator, and the two cases that the sūrah has already given a hearing, the old man and the barren woman, are folded into one sentence. The girl is told that the thing she cannot imagine belongs to a category of acts that her Lord does whenever He wills.

The reply continues with the words that have been repeated in every generation since: ***“When He decrees a matter, He simply tells it, ‘Be!’”*** and ***“And it is”***. Ibn Kathīr explains that the decree of a thing is followed by its existence without a delay and without the need for a cause, and (3:59 — **“then said to him, “Be!” And he was”**) gives the same principle for the creation of Adam. The verse therefore places the birth of ʿĪsā in the class of God's acts rather than in a class of its own, and that is exactly what the argument with the delegation needed.

**WHAT THE ANSWER SETTLES FOR THE READER**

The exchange answers a question about means by naming the agent, and the two questions asked in this sūrah now stand beside each other: the old man with a barren wife, and the young woman with no husband. Both received a birth, and both received the same reply, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson that the door of created things is opened by the will of God rather than by the state of the household. A person who reads the two stories together cannot claim a power for either child.

Think of two locked doors in the same wall, one rusted shut with age and one that has never had a key made for it. The verse reports that both were opened by the same word, and it leaves the demonstration standing in the text. What follows for the reader who is arguing about the nature of ʿĪsā is that the manner of his birth points to the One who commanded it rather than to the one who was born. The sentence that closes the verse is the same sentence that will close the argument of the whole passage.

---

## Verse 3:48

> And Allah will teach him writing and wisdom, the Torah and the Gospel

**WHAT THE CHILD WOULD BE TAUGHT**

The announcement turns from the birth to the education: ***“And Allah will teach him writing”*** and ***“and wisdom, the Torah”*** and ***“and the Gospel”***. Ibn ʿAbbās explains the first item as the writings of the prophets and the second as the knowledge of what is lawful and what is forbidden, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same list. So the man who would speak from a cradle would also sit as a student, and the teacher is named before the mission is. A prophet is taught before he teaches, and the verse puts the teaching first.

Ibn ʿAbbās records the older reading that the Torah was taught to him before he was born and the Gospel after, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the reports about what the writing means, including the reading that takes it as the art of writing itself. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes the place the two books have in the list, since the man was to be sent to the Children of Israel and would carry the earlier revelation as well as the new one. The education is therefore fitted to the audience, and the verse shows that the message and the messenger were prepared together.

**THE ORDER OF THE FOUR ITEMS**

The four things are given in an order that climbs: writing, wisdom, the Torah, the Gospel. The first is a skill, the second a judgment, the third an earlier book, and the fourth the book that would be given through him. Ibn Kathīr reads the list as the account of the knowledge ʿĪsā possessed, and the arrangement teaches that the skill served the wisdom and the wisdom served the two books. A man who can read but does not judge will not carry a revelation, and a man who judges without the skill cannot preserve one.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the consequence for the argument with the delegation, since the man who had been taught the Torah and the Gospel could not have come to abolish what the earlier book contained. The sūrah will say in the next verses that he came to confirm the Torah, and the list here is already making that point. A teacher who is formed by the books he is sent to confirm has no quarrel with them, and the formation is named before the mission.

**WHAT THE VERSE SAYS ABOUT KNOWLEDGE**

The verse is a small statement about the way knowledge is given, and it holds a lesson that reaches beyond the story. Everything the messenger would carry was taught to him, and the Book names the teacher as God. A person who receives knowledge this way is a servant rather than a partner, and the sūrah has been answering a claim about partnership from its opening. The list is the quiet refutation, and it lies inside the announcement of the child's greatness.

Think of a young man who is given a workshop, and who finds that the tools and the training were arranged for him years before he arrived. The verse describes the arrangement for ʿĪsā, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes the practical shape of it: the teaching came first and the message came after. What follows for the reader is a settled view of the man at the centre of the story, and a settled view of the One who formed him. The next verses send him to his people with the message his education had prepared.

---

## Verse 3:49

> and ˹make him˺ a messenger to the Children of Israel ˹to proclaim,˺ ‘I have come to you with a sign from your Lord: I will make for you a bird from clay, breathe into it, and it will become a ˹real˺ bird—by Allah’s Will. I will heal the blind and the leper and raise the dead to life—by Allah’s Will. And I will prophesize what you eat and store in your houses. Surely in this is a sign for you if you ˹truly˺ believe

**THE MESSAGE AND THE PEOPLE SENT TO**

The verse sends the child to his people: ***“and ˹make him˺ a messenger to the Children of Israel ˹to proclaim,˺ ‘I have come to you with a sign from your Lord”***. Ibn ʿAbbās notes that the mission began in his thirties, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading of the age. The address is to the Children of Israel by name, which means the first mission was not to the world but to the people who held the covenant and the books. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān observes that the signs that follow were aimed at an audience that asked for proofs, and the verse gives them proofs of a kind their own scriptures had taught them to expect.

**THE BIRD FROM CLAY**

The first sign is ***“I will make for you a bird from clay, breathe into it”*** and ***“and it will become a ˹real˺ bird”*** and ***“by Allah's Will. I will heal the blind”***. Ibn ʿAbbās describes the making of the shape and the breathing into it, and al-Jalālayn keeps the report. The words by Allah's Will are attached to each sign, and that is the point of the whole list. The man did not act on his own authority, and the words keep the reader from mistaking the servant for the power that answered him.

Ibn Kathīr reads the making of the bird as one of the proofs of the mission, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān counts the signs of the passage and notes that each one answers something the Children of Israel valued: the creation of life, the healing of the body, the raising of the dead, and the knowledge of what was hidden. The list is arranged so that no one of them can be explained away as a natural event. A prophet who makes a bird, heals a leper and raises the dead is doing what the Book says his Lord does, with permission.

**THE HEALING AND THE RAISING**

The list continues: ***“and the leper and raise the dead to life”*** and ***“by Allah's Will. And I will prophesize what you eat”*** and ***“and store in your houses. Surely in this is a sign”*** and ***“for you if you ˹truly˺ believe”***. The last of the four signs is the announcement of what people ate in private and stored in their houses, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the older account of what the people asked and what they were told. The sign of knowledge is placed after the signs of power, and the two together cover what a person fears and what a person hides.

Then the sentence turns to the condition: ***“for you if you ˹truly˺ believe”***. The signs were given to a people who asked for them, and the benefit named here depends on the reception. Al-Ṭabarī keeps the reports of the mission and the proofs, and the pattern that rises from the passage is that a proof is not the same as a decision. The sūrah will go on to describe what happened when the proofs came, and the reader who has followed the story knows what the answer was.

**WHAT THE SIGNS WERE FOR**

The five signs are held together in the verse by one phrase that repeats: by Allah's Will. The repetition is the teaching, and it is placed after every act so that the reader cannot slip past it. Everything ʿĪsā did was done with permission, and the verse says so before the children of Israel are asked to believe. The claim at stake between the sūrah and the delegation was about the nature of the man, and the list of his works is drawn so that the servant-hood of the man is visible in every one of them.

Think of a king's envoy who arrives at a city with a signed letter and works wonders that only the king's seal could authorise. The verse presents the envoy with the seal attached to each of his acts, and then asks the city to read the letter. What follows for the reader is a view of the man in the story that keeps him honoured and keeps him a servant, and the verse reaches that balance by naming his works and their author in the same breath.

---

## Verse 3:50

> And I will confirm the Torah revealed before me and legalize some of what had been forbidden to you. I have come to you with a sign from your Lord, so be mindful of Allah and obey me

**CONFIRMING WHAT CAME BEFORE**

The address to the Children of Israel settles the mission's relation to their book: ***“And I will confirm the Torah revealed before me”*** and ***“and legalize some of what had been forbidden to you. I have come to you with a sign from your Lord”***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the confirming as the agreement of the message with the oneness of God that the Torah carried, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense and adds what was made lawful. So the man came neither to erase the book of Mūsā nor to leave it exactly as it had been applied, and the sentence carries the two together.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws out the role of the confirming in the argument with the delegation, since a messenger who confirms the earlier revelation cannot be set against it. Ibn Kathīr keeps the same point, and (5:46 — **“We sent Jesus, son of Mary, confirming the Torah revealed before him”**) states the mission in the same words. The sūrah's case against the claim of divinity rests partly on this: a man who came to confirm a book that taught the oneness of God cannot have come to introduce a partner into it.

**THE LAWFUL AND THE FORBIDDEN**

The sentence then names the relief the message carried: ***“and legalize some of what had been forbidden to you”***. Ibn ʿAbbās lists what was made lawful, including the meat of camels and the fat of cattle and sheep, and al-Jalālayn records the older reading that the burden was lifted in full. The two readings differ over the extent and agree that the change came from God rather than from the messenger's own will. A prophet who loosens a rule carries the authority for the loosening, and the sentence gives that authority back to its source at once.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes the effect of the sentence on the audience, since the people addressed had been living under a heavy law and were being told that part of the weight would be lifted. The change was a mercy, and it was a test as well, because a community that receives a lighter law is being asked whether it will use the room or fall into negligence. The verse places the relief beside the confirming, so that the two are seen as one act of God.

**THE COMMAND THAT CLOSES THE ADDRESS**

The verse ends with ***“so be mindful of Allah”*** and ***“and obey me”***. The two commands are joined, and the order is the whole message of the passage. Mindfulness of God comes first, and obedience to the messenger follows it, because the messenger has just said that his works were done by God's will and that his message confirms God's book. A command to obey a man is placed at the end of a speech in which the man credits every act to his Lord, and that arrangement is the difference between a prophet and a claimant.

Think of a foreman who is sent to a site with the owner's plans, who shows the plans to the workers, and who then asks them to follow his instructions on the site. Nobody mistakes the foreman for the owner, and al-Saʿdī reads the passage as the same kind of arrangement. The verse closes the address in that shape, and it does so with a command that every reader of the Book can carry: remember the One who sent the message, and follow the one who brought it.

---

## Verse 3:51

> Surely Allah is my Lord and your Lord. So worship Him ˹alone˺. This is the Straight Path.’”

**THE SUMMARY OF THE MESSAGE**

The address to the Children of Israel closes with a sentence that gathers the whole mission: ***“Surely Allah is my Lord”*** and ***“and your Lord. So worship Him ˹alone˺. This is the Straight Path.’””***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the words as the call to declare God's oneness, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense and adds that the messenger's own confession is placed first. The man who had spoken from a cradle and healed the sick stands in the same line as his hearers and names the same Lord. A prophet who says my Lord and your Lord in one breath has settled the question of who is above him.

The sentence then names the duty that follows: ***“So worship Him ˹alone˺”***. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the sequence out, since the confession of the Lord comes before the command to worship, and the command is what the confession asks of a life. The people had asked for signs, and the signs had been given; now the verse tells them what the signs were for. A proof that ends in admiration has not finished its work, and the message of ʿĪsā ends in the word worship.

**THE PATH THAT IS NAMED LAST**

The final words are ***“This is the Straight Path.’””***. Ibn ʿAbbās explains it as the religion that God is pleased with, and the description is the same one the sūrah will apply to the community of Muḥammad in a later passage. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes the sameness, and the sameness is the point of the whole address. The road taught by ʿĪsā and the road taught by the messenger of Madīnah are one road, and the verse gives the earlier prophet the words that describe it.

Ibn Kathīr keeps the sentence in the sequence of the mission, al-Ṭabarī records the reports, and the accounts agree that the people rejected the call after hearing it. That is why the verse is placed where it is: the message has been stated completely, and only then does the sūrah describe what the people did with it. Think of a road sign that is posted at a fork and then ignored by half the drivers; the sign is not at fault, and the Book records the sign before it records the choice.

**WHAT THE VERSE SETTLES FOR THE READER**

The verse is short, and it holds three things that the sūrah has been building toward. The man is a servant with a Lord, which answers the claim about his nature. The duty is the worship of God alone, which is the same duty every messenger brought. The path is one, which answers the community that wanted to divide the prophets into rival camps. The words are put in the mouth of ʿĪsā himself, and al-Jalālayn notes that his people rejected him after this speech rather than before it.

What follows for the reader is a plain measure of the man at the centre of the story. He did not ask to be worshipped, and he did not ask for a share in the divinity. He named his Lord and the Lord of his hearers, and he called them to the worship of One. The verse is the closest thing in the sūrah to a summary of the message that the delegation from Najran had come to argue about, and it is stated by the man they honoured before the sūrah says a word about his birth.

---

## Verse 3:52

> When Jesus sensed disbelief from his people, he asked, “Who will stand up with me for Allah?” The disciples replied, “We will stand up for Allah. We believe in Allah, so bear witness that we have submitted.”

**THE DISBELIEF THAT WAS SENSED**

The scene turns to the response: ***“When Jesus sensed disbelief from his people, he asked, “Who will stand up with me”*** and ***“for Allah?” The disciples replied, “We will stand up”***. Al-Qurṭubī explains the sensing as a knowing and a finding, since the man had come to see that the refusal was settled rather than temporary, and Ibn ʿAbbās records that the hostility had reached the point of a plan against his life. The question is therefore not a request for information. It is the call of a man who has understood what is coming.

Al-Jalālayn explains who the disciples were: the closest companions of ʿĪsā and the first to believe in him, twelve men of pure intent, and he records the older explanations of the title they carry. Ibn Kathīr keeps the same material and adds the point that their answer was immediate. So the verse sets two groups against each other in one sentence, the people who refused and the small company that stood, and the sūrah has been making the same contrast since its opening passage about the two kinds of heart.

**THE QUESTION THAT ASKS FOR ALLIES**

The question is ***“Who will stand up with me”*** and ***“for Allah?”***. The helpers are those who would stand against the enemies of God, as Ibn ʿAbbās reads the phrase, and the help is tied to God rather than to the man. A person who answers such a call is not joining a faction. He is taking a side in a matter that belongs to his Lord, and the wording protects the cause from becoming a personal loyalty. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson from this for the community of the later messenger, since the same call is repeated in the Book for a later generation.

The disciples' answer is given in full: ***“We will stand up”*** and ***“for Allah. We believe in Allah”*** and ***“so bear witness that we have submitted.””***. The last word is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as the acknowledgement of God in worship and in the declaration of His oneness, and al-Jalālayn explains the same. So their answer contains three parts: a promise of help, a confession of faith, and a request that the prophet witness their submission. The order puts the action before the testimony, which is the way a claim of faith is proven.

**WHAT THE SCENE SHOWS THE COMMUNITY**

The scene is set in a place far from Madīnah and long before the sūrah, and it is told to a community under pressure. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the passage identifies the first followers of ʿĪsā and brings out the creed they held, which is the same creed the sūrah is defending. A small band standing with a prophet against his own people is a picture the listeners in Madīnah could read directly, since they were a small band themselves, and the verse gives them the words the earlier band used.

Think of a village where a single household refuses to join a feast that has become a wrong, and where the household says so in public and then asks the guest of honour to remember it. The verse shows the disciples doing exactly that with their prophet, and it records the sentence they spoke. The sūrah will name their prayer in the next verse and their reward in the next chapter, and the sequence begins with the plainest possible answer to a plain question: who will stand.

---

## Verse 3:53

> ˹They prayed to Allah,˺ “Our Lord! We believe in Your revelations and follow the messenger, so count us among those who bear witness.”

**THE PRAYER THAT FOLLOWED THE ANSWER**

The disciples' declaration turns into a prayer: ***“˹They prayed to Allah,˺ “Our Lord!”*** and ***“We believe in Your revelations”*** and ***“and follow the messenger”***. Al-Ṭabarī reads the passage as the report of what the companions of ʿĪsā said, and the order of the words matters. Faith is named first, the following of the messenger second, and the request third. A person who makes this prayer has already put his belief and his walk in front of his request, and the verse keeps that order for the reader.

Ibn ʿAbbās explains the revelations as the Gospel sent down and the judgments God had made plain, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense and identifies the messenger as ʿĪsā. So the prayer names the book and the man, and the two are held together in one sentence. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes the same pairing and draws the lesson that faith in a revelation is shown by following the one who brought it. The prayer is the disciples' own summary of what the previous verse had promised in words, and that is why the two verses are read together. Since the following of the messenger is named inside the prayer, the claim to faith is worked out in the way a person walks rather than in the words he repeats.

**THE REQUEST TO JOIN THE WITNESSES**

The prayer ends with ***“so count us among those”*** and ***“who bear witness.””***. Ibn ʿAbbās gives the older explanations of the witnesses, including the reading that they are the forerunners who testified before them, and the reading that they belong to the community of the later messenger. Al-Qurṭubī keeps both readings and notes that the request is to be enrolled in a company rather than to receive a personal honour. The disciples did not ask to be singled out. They asked to be counted.

That distinction is the whole texture of the verse. A group that had answered the call of a prophet with their lives asks to be recorded as one item in a list of truthful witnesses, and the Book keeps the request. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the meaning out for every reader, since the ambition of a believer is not a rank above others but a place inside the truth. Think of a registrar who is asked by a labourer to write him into the roll of men who worked on a difficult bridge; the request is for the work, not for the name.

**WHAT THE VERSE TEACHES ABOUT TESTIMONY**

The word that closes the prayer is the key to the passage, and it links the disciples to the witnesses named earlier in the sūrah. Al-Ṭabarī notes the connection with the testimony of God, the angels and the people of knowledge in an earlier verse, and the same word is used for both. The disciples are asking to be enrolled in the company that bears witness to the truth, and the sūrah has already shown that this is the finest company a person can join.

The verse also answers a question the reader may carry about the small band of followers. They were few, they were not in power, and their prophet was under threat from his own people. The prayer they made was not for victory, for safety, or for a sign. It was to be recorded among the witnesses, and the Book preserves the sentence as the model of what a follower should ask for when the cause is hard. What follows is a promise of protection that the next verses will give, and the reader sees the two together: the prayer for a place among the witnesses, and the answer of the One who never loses a witness.

---

## Verse 3:54

> And the disbelievers made a plan ˹against Jesus˺, but Allah also planned—and Allah is the best of planners

**THE PLAN AND THE COUNTER-PLAN**

The verse takes the two sides of the struggle and names both: ***“And the disbelievers made a plan ˹against Jesus˺”*** and ***“but Allah also planned”*** and ***“and Allah is the best of planners”***. Ibn Kathīr reads the plot as the design a group of the Children of Israel formed against the prophet, and the reports describe the manhunt and the arrangement that was meant to end the mission. The same word is used for both sides in one sentence, so that the reader can weigh the two designs.

Al-Jalālayn notes the occasion and the fact that the plan was aimed at killing him, al-Qurṭubī keeps the older reports of the night in question, and al-Saʿdī reads the passage as the assurance that a plot against a prophet cannot outrun its Lord. What turns on the pairing is that God does not merely prevent a plot; He works one of His own. The sūrah will say in the next verse what the plan of God was, and the reader is told to expect a design that the plotters could not see. A hand that only blocks a blow is a shield, and something larger is described here. That is why the comparison is made at all, since a reader who understands the two plans stops measuring the danger by the number of the plotters.

**THE NAME THAT SETTLES THE COMPARISON**

The sentence closes with ***“and Allah is the best of planners”***. Ibn Kathīr reads the phrase as the statement that God's arrangement is better in knowledge and stronger in outcome, and the sense of the opening word is a comparison. Two designs exist, and one is drawn by men with what they know of the present, while the other belongs to the One who knows the ends of every road. The verse does not say the plotters were clumsy. The plan they made was matched by one that was better.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes the same word and the same comparison, and he draws the practical lesson for the community that reads the sūrah under pressure of its own. Plots are made against believers in every age, and the assurance given here is not that the plots do not happen. It is that the One who holds the end of the story also holds a plan. Think of a weaver who watches a hand reach for one of his threads; the hand may take the thread, and the cloth is still being made by the man who knows the whole design.

**THE PATTERN THE SŪRAH SETS**

The verse is placed between the prayer of the disciples and the promise given to ʿĪsā, and the position makes it a hinge. The readers of the sūrah in Madīnah had their own plots to face, and the passage tells them what to expect and where to look. The plan against the prophet of God was made by people who held the city and the temple, and it failed in a way that no one in the story could have predicted. The next verse gives the words of God to ʿĪsā about it, and those words are the answer to the plan.

The sūrah does not tell the story of the night in detail, and al-Qurṭubī notes the reports that surround it. What the Book does is name the two plans and leave the comparison to the sentence at the end. A reader who has followed the story from the prayer of Ḥannah to this point has seen a barren woman given a child, an old man given a son, and a virgin given a prophet, and the verse before him says that the men who plotted against that prophet were dealing with the same power. What follows is the sentence where the plan of God is stated in full.

---

## Verse 3:55

> ˹Remember˺ when Allah said, “O Jesus! I will take you and raise you up to Myself. I will deliver you from those who disbelieve, and elevate your followers above the disbelievers until the Day of Judgment. Then to Me you will ˹all˺ return, and I will settle all your disputes

**THE ADDRESS AND THE TAKING UP**

The plan of God is stated as a promise: ***“˹Remember˺ when Allah said, “O Jesus!”*** and ***“I will take you”*** and ***“and raise you up to Myself. I will deliver you from those”***. Al-Ṭabarī works through the readings of the first verb, since the taking has been understood as the receiving of the man at the end of his term and has also been linked to the sleep of a living person, and the reports differ over the details while agreeing that the threat was not allowed to close the mission. The plain sense that the man was taken and lifted is kept by Ibn ʿAbbās, and al-Qurṭubī records the older discussions of the passage.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes the way the promise is arranged: the taking, the raising, the deliverance, and the elevation of the followers. The reader watches the story arrive at the point where the plan of the plotters meets its limit, and the limit is the sentence that begins with the word I. Everything that follows in the verse is a promise made by God to a servant who was about to be left alone, and the voice heard in the passage belongs to the One who arranged the whole matter.

**THE FOLLOWERS WHO ARE LIFTED**

The sentence continues: ***“who disbelieve, and elevate your followers above the disbelievers”*** and ***“until the Day of Judgment.”***. Ibn Kathīr reads the elevation as the victory of the believing community over those who denied, and he records the readings that explain it as the standing of the truth rather than the fate of individuals. Al-Jalālayn keeps the same line and notes the term that extends the promise to the last day. What follows is that the promise is not a description of a single afternoon in Jerusalem. It is a statement about the road of the community that carried the message.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws out the practical edge for the readers of the sūrah, since they were themselves part of the followers mentioned in the sentence. The community of Madīnah was a small band among larger and better-armed neighbours, and the promise that the followers of ʿĪsā would be lifted above the disbelievers was read by them as a word about their own case as well. The Book often gives a story and a promise together, and the promise here is answered by the community that later received the last messenger.

**THE RETURN AND THE SETTLEMENT**

The verse closes with ***“Then to Me you will ˹all˺ return”*** and ***“and I will settle all your disputes”***. The return is addressed to all the parties in the story, and the word that follows is the settlement of differences. Al-Ṭabarī keeps the same reading, and al-Qurṭubī notes that the disputes named are the disagreements about the man himself, which the sūrah has been answering from its opening. The sentence therefore ends the story where the argument has to end, in the hands of the One who knows what every side said and what every side intended.

Think of a court that hears a case in which the witnesses contradict one another for years, and then finds that the maker of the contract is the judge. Nobody in the room doubts that the account will be settled, and the sentence in the verse gives that settlement to God. What follows for the reader is a trust that carries him past the confusion of the arguments, and the next two verses name what the settlement will look like for each group. The promise to ʿĪsā is finished, and the account is handed over.

---

## Verse 3:56

> As for those who disbelieve, I will subject them to a severe punishment in this life and the Hereafter, and they will have no helpers

**THE PUNISHMENT NAMED FOR THE REFUSERS**

The settlement begins with the group that refused: ***“As for those who disbelieve, I will subject them to a severe punishment in this life”*** and ***“and the Hereafter, and they will have no helpers”***. Ibn Kathīr reads the punishment of this life as the humiliation and defeat that comes with a rejected message, and the punishment of the next as the fire, and he keeps the two together. The verse gives both worlds in one sentence, which is the way the Book treats a refusal that has been shown the proof.

Al-Jalālayn keeps the same reading in a short line, and al-Qurṭubī notes the older reports on the two punishments. The people addressed had chased a prophet and planned his death, and the sentence they receive names the cost in the life they were still living and in the one that follows it. A punishment that arrives in both worlds cannot be escaped by dying, and that is the point of naming the two together.

**THE HELPERS WHO WILL NOT BE THERE**

The verse then takes up the helpers and removes them: ***“and they will have no helpers”***. The same ending has appeared before in the sūrah, where the wealth and the children of the disbelievers could not help them, and the repetition is deliberate. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the plain sense, and the whole line of the passage comes back to the same point: the resources a person gathers in this life cannot enter the account on his behalf. The men who plotted against ʿĪsā had the temple, the city and the law on their side, and the day is named here when none of that answers.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes the contrast the passage builds with the disciples, who a few verses earlier asked to be counted among the witnesses. One group gathered allies, and the other asked for a place in the record, and the verse closes the first group's account with the absence of helpers. Think of a man who fills a courtroom with supporters and then finds that the judgment is read to him alone. The sentence in the verse describes exactly that room, and the reader is standing outside it.

**WHAT THE WARNING SAYS TO THE READER**

The verse is addressed to a group long dead, and the sūrah tells it to a living community, and the distance is the teaching. Al-Ṭabarī keeps the passage in the context of the delegation from Najran, which had come to argue about ʿĪsā and had heard his own words quoted back to them. The warning is placed after the promise to the man they honoured, and the arrangement is exact: the prophet is honoured and raised, and the people who denied him are answered.

What follows for the reader is a question about where he stands in the two lists. The next verse names the second list in full, and the two are kept side by side in the sūrah so that no reader has to guess which one he is in. The warning is severe, and it is placed next to a promise, and the Book uses the pairing because the two answers are given to the same hearing.

---

## Verse 3:57

> And as for those who believe and do good, they will be rewarded in full. And Allah does not like the wrongdoers.”

**THE SECOND LIST AND ITS CONDITION**

The settlement turns to the other group: ***“And as for those”***, the people ***“who believe and do good, they will be rewarded in full.”***. Ibn Kathīr reads the sentence as the promise of full payment for the people who joined faith to action, and the pairing of the two words is the condition. Belief alone is not the description, and work alone is not the description. The two are named together, and the promise is attached to both of them.

Al-Qurṭubī notes the older reports on the reward. ***“they will be rewarded in full.”*** is then explained as a payment in which nothing is deducted and nothing is delayed, and the sūrah has used the same word for the day when every soul is paid in full. So the verse places the reward of the believers on the same measure as the account of the last day, and the reader is told that the settlement he will meet is not a partial one.

**THE SENTENCE THAT CLOSES THE PAIRING**

The verse ends with ***“And Allah does not like the wrongdoers.””***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the wrongdoers as the people who exceeded the limit, and the sentence closes the two lists by naming the quality that separated them. Al-Ṭabarī keeps the same reading, and the point of the ending is that the difference between the two groups was never a matter of information. Both had heard the message, and one group wronged the messenger and the other followed him.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson for the reader who finds himself between the two lists, since the verse gives the way in rather than a verdict on a people. A person who believes and does good is inside the promise, and the sentence that follows warns him away from the road that would take him out of it. The pairing of the promise with a warning about wrongdoers is therefore an invitation as much as a verdict, and the Book often ends a passage this way.

**WHAT THE TWO LISTS LEAVE WITH THE READER**

The two verses are read together in the sūrah, and they cover every hearer of the story. One group refused the prophet and planned against him, and their account ends with a punishment in two worlds and no helpers. The other group believed and acted, and their account ends with a payment in full. The prophet at the centre of the story was himself a servant, and the two roads that meet at his feet are the same two roads the sūrah has been describing since it named the God-fearing and the disbelievers at its start.

Think of a road that forks at a signpost, where one path leads to a washed-out bridge and the other to a town. The verse puts the two destinations on the sign and leaves the traveller standing at the fork, and the Book does this in every generation because the fork is dug anew in every life. The next verses turn to the reader of the Book itself, and they name the revelation that has been carrying the whole story.

---

## Verse 3:58

> We recite ˹all˺ this to you ˹O Prophet˺ as one of the signs and ˹as˺ a wise reminder

**THE RECITATION AND ITS SOURCE**

The story pauses to name what the reader has been following, and the naming comes from God: ***“We recite ˹all˺ this to you ˹O Prophet˺”***. Al-Ṭabarī reads the sentence as the statement that the whole account has been recited to the messenger, and the word we places the recitation in the hands of its owner. The passage is not a history the messenger gathered from the people of the Book. It is a recitation delivered to him, and the account says so at the point where the story is most contested.

Ibn Kathīr keeps the same reading and notes that the recitation covers the stories of Zachariah, Maryam and ʿĪsā together with the arguments that surround them. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the proof of prophethood from the sentence, since the details of the story were not available to a man in Madīnah. The account is tied to the revelation rather than to a chain of teachers, and the tying is done in the middle of the argument rather than at its start.

**THE SIGN AND THE WISE REMINDER**

The sentence then names what the account is for the reader: ***“as one of the signs”*** and ***“and ˹as˺ a wise reminder”***. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the older reading of the second phrase, and al-Jalālayn notes the same sense. A reminder brings back what a person already knows at heart, and wisdom is the arrangement of the material so that the hearer can act on it. So the story of the family has not been told for entertainment and not only to settle a dispute. The story is told to remind the hearer of the oneness of God and to move him.

Al-Qurṭubī notes the place of the verse in the flow of the sūrah, since it arrives after the promise given to ʿĪsā and before the verse that names the likeness of the two men. The reminder is therefore pointed at a specific claim, and the Book places the reminder where the claim can be seen. A wise teacher repeats the principle at the moment the student is about to need it, and that is what happens here.

**WHAT THE SENTENCE TELLS THE LISTENER**

The sentence gives the reader two things to hold. The account came by recitation, which settles the question of where it came from, and it is a sign and a reminder, which settles the question of why it is being told. A person who treats the passage as a chronicle of old events will miss the second half of the sentence, and the Book states the purpose before it goes on with the argument.

Think of a physician who reads a case history to a patient and then explains why that case was chosen. The same thing happens here: the material and the purpose are named, and then the argument continues. What follows is the sentence the whole passage has been building toward, in which the likeness of ʿĪsā is named and the dispute with the delegation is settled in a few words.

---

## Verse 3:59

> Indeed, the example of Jesus in the sight of Allah is like that of Adam. He created him from dust, then said to him, “Be!” And he was

**THE LIKENESS THAT SETTLES THE ARGUMENT**

The sūrah states the comparison directly: ***“Indeed, the example of Jesus in the sight of Allah is like that of Adam.”***. Ibn Kathīr reads the sentence as the answer to the claim that a birth without a father is a proof of divinity, and the comparison is drawn from the earlier story the delegation itself honoured. Adam had no father and no mother, and no one ever thought of him as a partner of God. A person who accepts the creation of Adam without a father has already accepted the possibility of a creation without a father, and that acceptance is what the argument turns on.

The same line is kept in a short sentence by al-Jalālayn, and al-Qurṭubī records the older reports on the passage. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws out the force of the argument, which is that the unusual manner of a birth says something about the power of the Creator and nothing about the rank of the created. The claim of the delegation rested on a manner of coming into being, and the verse answers it by naming a manner more unusual still.

**THE COMMAND AND THE BEING**

The sentence continues with the way Adam came to be: ***“He created him from dust, then said to him, “Be!” And he was”***. The sequence of the creation from dust and the command that followed it is kept by Ibn ʿAbbās, and Ibn Kathīr explains that the command was enough. The verse gives no process and no material beyond the dust, and the point of the sentence is that a word from God is sufficient for a being. The same word is what Maryam was told about her child, and the two stories are joined by the verse rather than by a chain of reasoning.

The older explanations of the passage are kept by al-Ṭabarī, and the reports agree that the likeness is complete. Two men, neither of whom had an ordinary beginning, and one of them is the father of the human race. If the manner of a beginning were the measure of divinity, the first man would have had the stronger claim, and no community claimed it for him. The verse leaves the delegation with that comparison and does not need a second argument.

**WHAT THE VERSE TEACHES ABOUT LIKENESS AND RANK**

The passage teaches something that reaches past the dispute, since it shows how a person should weigh a wonder. A wonder is a sign of the power of the One who made it, and it is not a rank held by the one through whom it appeared. Think of a king who builds two doors into the same hall, one from timber and one from stone. The door made of stone is not more royal than the other, because the king made both. The verse draws the comparison of the two beginnings so that the reader can see the two doors in one wall.

The difference between a likeness and an equality is noted by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, since the example is said to be like the other and the two men are not said to be the same. Adam and ʿĪsā are alike in the manner of their coming and distinct in everything else, and the sūrah has already given ʿĪsā his own rank among the honoured and the brought-near. What follows is a verse that turns from the argument to the messenger who carries it, and it warns him against the company of doubters.

---

## Verse 3:60

> This is the truth from your Lord, so do not be one of those who doubt

**THE TRUTH AND ITS SOURCE**

The argument closes with a declaration and a warning: ***“This is the truth from your Lord”*** and ***“so do not be one of those who doubt”***. Al-Ṭabarī reads the first sentence as the confirmation of everything the passage has said about ʿĪsā, and the source is named in the words from your Lord. So the truth of the matter is not a conclusion the community reached and not a position one party won. It came from God, and the sentence names the owner of the claim.

Ibn Kathīr keeps the same reading and notes that the address is given to the messenger, which means the passage is spoken to a man who was never in doubt. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson from that arrangement, since the command is a form of strengthening rather than a correction. A commander who tells his soldier not to be afraid is not accusing him of cowardice. He is naming the ground the soldier stands on, and the ground is the truth from his Lord.

**THE WARNING ABOUT THE COMPANY OF DOUBT**

The second sentence is a warning about company: ***“so do not be one of those who doubt”***. Al-Jalālayn keeps the plain sense, and al-Qurṭubī notes the older discussion of the command and the person addressed. The warning is not about holding a question; it is about being one of the doubters, which is a statement about where a person stands. A man may come near a group with his own answers and end up sharing its questions, and that is why the warning is phrased as it is. The sentence turns the messenger's face away from the circle, since the standing matters and not the opinion alone.

Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same reading in the older wording. The sūrah has been arguing with a delegation that came to trade questions, and the closing words turn the messenger toward the truth he was given. Delegations of every age arrive with the same technique, and the Book gives the same counsel: state the truth, answer the question, and then do not take up residence in the middle of the doubt.

**WHAT THE VERSE LEAVES WITH THE READER**

The verse ends the argument about ʿĪsā, and it does so with a sentence a reader can carry into any dispute of his own. The truth is named as coming from God, and the warning is named as a matter of standing rather than of opinion. The two parts belong together, since a person who has the truth does not need the approval of the doubters and gains nothing by staying in their circle. A man may win every argument in a room and lose his footing in it.

Think of a traveller who has found the right road after a long search and then meets a group of men arguing at the crossroads about whether any road exists. He states what he knows and keeps walking, and the next verses show what the messenger did with the delegations that came to him. The sūrah is about to give the community the most public instrument in the whole passage, which is the invitation to settle the matter by invoking the curse of God on the side that lies.

---

## Verse 3:61

> Now, whoever disputes with you ˹O Prophet˺ concerning Jesus after full knowledge has come to you, say, “Come! Let us gather our children and your children, our women and your women, ourselves and yourselves—then let us sincerely invoke Allah’s curse upon the liars.”

**THE DISPUTE THAT WOULD NOT END**

The argument reaches its last instrument: ***“Now, whoever disputes with you ˹O Prophet˺ concerning Jesus after full knowledge has come to you, say, “Come!”***. Al-Ṭabarī reads the sentence as the answer to anyone who continues to argue after the case has been made, and al-Qurṭubī notes that the knowledge named in the passage is the knowledge that ʿĪsā was the servant and messenger of God. Ibn ʿAbbās records the dispute of the delegation of Najran, which held to its position after the likeness of Adam had been given to it. The verse therefore comes at the point where argument has stopped producing anything.

Al-Jalālayn keeps the account of the delegation and adds what happened when the invitation was given. The men asked for time to consider, and one of them, who was respected among them, told them that they knew the man was a prophet and that no people had ever challenged a prophet to this and survived it. The verse is a public test, and the people it was offered to understood exactly what it meant, which is why their answer is recorded as a request for time rather than a refusal.

**THE SUMMONS THAT INCLUDES EVERYONE**

The invitation names the families in pairs: ***“Let us gather our children”*** and ***“and your children, our women”*** and ***“and your women, ourselves”*** and ***“and yourselves then let us sincerely invoke Allah’s curse upon the liars.””***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the pairs as the summoning of the two households, so that no one on either side stands apart from the question. The proposal is not a duel between two learned men. It brings the children and the women, which means both sides put everything they love on the table.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān explains the practice, which the scholars call the mutual invocation, and gives its sense: when the arguments fail, the two parties ask God together to place His curse on whichever side is lying, and the curse means being driven away from mercy. The logic is exact, since a person who is certain of his case does not fear such a prayer and a person who is not does. The verse therefore turns a debate into a test of sincerity, and it leaves the outcome in the hands of God.

**WHAT THE PROPOSAL REVEALS**

The proposal tells the reader what the messenger thought of his own case. al-Jalālayn records the names of those who went out with him for the meeting, and the reports agree that he came with the members of his household. A man who brings his family to a prayer of this kind has no doubt about the answer, and the other side understood that. Their withdrawal is preserved in the reports and treated by the commentators as the moment the argument ended.

Think of two merchants in a dispute over a cargo, where one proposes that they both swear on their children's futures and the other asks for a day to think it over. The second request says everything the argument had not, and the sūrah records the same kind of pause. What follows for the reader is a settled view of the question that was at issue, since the side that proposed the test was willing to stake what the other side would not. The next verses state the conclusion of the matter plainly.

---

## Verse 3:62

> Certainly, this is the true narrative, and there is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Allah. And indeed, Allah ˹alone˺ is the Almighty, All-Wise

**THE TRUE NARRATIVE AND WHAT IT SETTLES**

The story is closed with a verdict: ***“Certainly, this is the true narrative”*** and ***“and there is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Allah.”***. Al-Ṭabarī reads the first sentence as the confirmation of everything told about ʿĪsā, and al-Qurṭubī notes that the word used for narrative carries the idea of a story told in sequence, since the meanings follow one another inside it. The verdict is placed after the account of the family and after the invitation, and the verdict covers the whole passage.

Ibn ʿAbbās reads the sentence as the statement that ʿĪsā was not God, not a son and not a partner, and the three denials are the exact questions the delegation had brought. So the verdict answers each of the points in turn, and it does so after the reader has heard the story of the cradle, the clay bird and the raising of the dead. The truth of the account and the truth about the man are one thing in the verse, and the sūrah keeps them together.

**THE DECLARATION THAT STANDS IN THE MIDDLE**

The sentence then returns to the declaration that opened the sūrah: ***“and there is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Allah.”***. Al-Jalālayn keeps the plain sense, and Ibn ʿAbbās notes that the words rule out a son and a partner at once. The sūrah has answered the delegation with a story, a likeness, and an invitation to a public prayer. Then it states the declaration again, and the repetition shows where the argument was always heading.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the order out, since the account is confirmed before the doctrine is stated, and the arrangement teaches the reader how the two belong together. The story is not an illustration of the doctrine. The doctrine is what the story establishes, and the sentence does the same at the end of the family's account. The order matters, since a verdict placed before the account would be a claim rather than a conclusion. A reader who has followed the passage from the prayer of Ḥannah has been shown the case rather than told it, and the sentence here gathers the case into a line.

**THE TWO NAMES AT THE CLOSE**

The verse ends with ***“And indeed, Allah ˹alone˺ is the Almighty, All-Wise”***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the first as the power that settles the matter and the second as the wisdom that arranged it. The two names have appeared before in the sūrah, and their return here marks the end of the argument as the beginning of the account's consequences. Power and wisdom together mean that the case is not merely stated but enforced, and the reading of the passage holds both.

Think of a judge who reads out the finding and then names the law under which it was reached. The two sentences belong to one act, and the verse ends the same way. What follows is a short verse about the people who turn away, and then the chapter turns from the discussion about ʿĪsā to an invitation addressed to the People of the Book as a whole, in which the same declaration is offered as a common ground.

---

## Verse 3:63

> If they turn away, then surely Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of the corruptors

**THE TURNING AWAY AND WHO KNOWS IT**

The verdict is followed by a short sentence about the people who refuse it: ***“If they turn away”*** and ***“then surely Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of the corruptors”***. Ibn Kathīr reads the passage as the statement that God is aware of those who make mischief, and he keeps the word used for the corruptors in its plain sense. The people addressed had been offered the argument, the likeness and the public test, and the sentence names what their refusal means rather than what it costs.

Al-Jalālayn keeps the sentence short, and al-Qurṭubī notes the reading of the passage. The word knowledge is placed where a reader might expect a punishment, and the choice is deliberate. A person who turns away from a proof is not necessarily struck down in the hour of his refusal, and the knowledge of the case is with God. The sentence leaves the timing open and closes the door on the idea that anything is unrecorded.

**WHY THE VERSE IS SHORT**

The verse is the shortest in the passage, and its brevity is part of the argument. The sūrah has spent a long passage on the family of ʿImrān, on the signs of ʿĪsā, and on the invitation to the public prayer. Now, when the answer is refusal, the Book gives one sentence and moves on. Al-Ṭabarī keeps the connection with the previous verse, and the effect is that the refusal is treated as a settled fact rather than as a new topic.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson for the community that carries the message, since the sentence belongs to the same spirit as the instruction given to the messenger earlier in the sūrah about his duty being to deliver. A person who has stated the case clearly has finished his work, and the rest belongs to the One who knows. The verse gives the messenger that rest, and it gives the reader the same counsel.

**WHAT THE SENTENCE LEAVES WITH THE READER**

The sentence names knowledge rather than wrath, and the choice is a mercy as well as a warning. A person who is turning away while he reads the passage is being told that the turning is seen and that the door is still open, since the One who knows is also the One who has been patient through the whole account. The verse does not slam a door. It leaves the reader with the knowledge that nothing about his position is hidden.

Think of a teacher who has explained a point three times, given the class a worked example, and offered a public test, and who then writes a single line in the record when a student walks out. The line is short because the case is complete, and the record is kept by someone who knows the student's reasons better than the student does. The sūrah turns from that line into a general invitation, and the invitation is addressed to the whole community of the Book rather than to the delegation alone.

---

## Verse 3:64

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “O People of the Book! Let us come to common terms: that we will worship none but Allah, associate none with Him, nor take one another as lords instead of Allah.” But if they turn away, then say, “Bear witness that we have submitted ˹to Allah alone˺.”

**THE INVITATION TO A COMMON WORD**

The sūrah widens its address: ***“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “O People of the Book!”*** and ***“Let us come to common terms”***. The two readings of the audience are recorded by al-Qurṭubī, with one group taking the address to the Christians of Najran and another taking it to the Jews of Madīnah, and the reports of the scholars show that both were meant. Ibn Kathīr reads the common term as an honest and fair word that does not favour either party, and he notes the older explanation that the word in this place means a complete sentence.

The wording is set out in al-Jalālayn: the phrase means a statement whose matter is upright between the two sides. The method for carrying a message to people who differ is set out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, since the invitation begins with the point that both sides already hold rather than with the list of differences. The Book treats the common ground as a door, and the verse opens with it.

**THE THREE CLAUSES OF THE TERM**

The common term is then spelled out: ***“that we will worship none”*** and ***“but Allah, associate none with Him”*** and ***“nor take one another”*** and ***“as lords instead of Allah.””***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the first two clauses as the declaration of God's oneness and the rejection of any partner, and the third as the refusal to obey a person in what amounts to a transgression against God. One further sense is that the last clause answers the practice of taking rabbis and monks as lords, as al-Jalālayn notes.

The three clauses are arranged so that the first two would be accepted by any believer in God, and the third draws the line at loyalty to human authorities over the command of God. The report of the letter sent to the Byzantine ruler is noted by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, where the invitation was made on the same point of oneness. The verse therefore gives the community a form of words for the widest possible audience, and the form is rooted in a matter on which all the parties could agree.

**THE ANSWER GIVEN WHEN THEY REFUSE**

The verse closes with the same refusal and the same response as the previous passage: ***“But if they turn away”*** and ***“then say, “Bear witness”*** and ***“that we have submitted ˹to Allah alone˺.””***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the sentence as the declaration that the community has surrendered to God and acknowledged His oneness, and the wording turns the refusal into a testimony rather than a defeat. The messenger is told to state his own position and call the others to witness it.

Think of a man who proposes a fair agreement to a group that will not sign it, and who then asks the room to note that he offered and that he stands by what he proposed. The verse does exactly that, and it does it in a form that the community still uses, since the words bear witness have been said by Muslims in the presence of others ever since. The next verse turns to the point that the People of the Book were arguing about among themselves, and it takes the argument apart with a date.

---

## Verse 3:65

> O People of the Book! Why do you argue about Abraham, while the Torah and the Gospel were not revealed until long after him? Do you not understand

**THE ARGUMENT ABOUT ABRAHAM**

The address continues, and it takes up a second dispute: ***“O People of the Book!”*** and ***“Why do you argue about Abraham”***. The question is read by al-Ṭabarī as the rebuke of people who argued about the religion of Ibrāhīm, and the claim that stood behind the argument is recorded by al-Qurṭubī, since each of the two communities claimed that Ibrāhīm belonged to its own way. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same account and notes that the argument had reached the point of naming him a Jew and a Christian.

What the claim ignored is the point taken up by al-Jalālayn, since the names Jew and Christian came into use only after the two books were revealed, and the man lived long before them. The verse therefore answers the argument with a date, and the date is the strongest kind of answer to a claim about lineage. The same point is drawn out by Maʿārif al-Qurʾān, which notes that the question is addressed to the communities that had the records in their own hands.

**THE DATE THAT SETTLES THE CLAIM**

The two books are then brought in by name: ***“while the Torah and the Gospel were not revealed”*** and ***“until long after him?”***. The passage is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as the statement that nothing in the two books supports the claim that Ibrāhīm was a Jew or a Christian, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the same reading. Since the books came after the man by a long stretch of time, the label could not have been his, and the verse asks the question in a way that any reader of the books can verify.

The argument was already void for another reason, as Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes, since a person's way is a matter of what he followed rather than of who later claimed him. The verse answers the claim on its own terms and then leaves it. That is the pattern the sūrah has been using from the opening: the Book grants what is true and answers what is not, and it does not need a crowd of arguments to do it.

**THE QUESTION THAT CLOSES THE VERSE**

The verse ends with ***“Do you not understand”***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the question as the call to see that the Torah and the Gospel do not say what the arguers claimed, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense with the words about seeing the falsehood of the claim. The question is not an insult. It is the opening of a door, since a person who stops to think about the date of the books can reach the answer himself.

Think of two families who argue over which of them a famous scholar belongs to, and the clerk who points out that the man died a century before either family came to the city. The argument cannot survive the calendar, and the same tool is used here. What follows is a verse that goes further and shows where the argument about knowledge itself was leading, and then a final statement about the man both communities wanted to claim.

---

> Then after distress, He sent down serenity in the form of drowsiness overcoming some of you, while others were disturbed by evil thoughts about Allah—the thoughts of ˹pre-Islamic˺ ignorance. They ask, “Do we have a say in the matter?” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “All matters are destined by Allah.” They conceal in their hearts what they do not reveal to you.
